Now we use the constraints on the possible anticanonical degrees of curves in
W_{0}
and
\widehat{W}
, together with the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle structure of
\pi_{0}
outside finitely many points of
Y_{0}
, to exclude five out of six possibilities for
Y_{0}
, and conclude that
Y_{0}\cong\mathbb{P}^{3}
and

Y\cong\operatorname{Bl}_{r\,\text{\it pts}}\mathbb{P}^{3}
(Lemma
5.54
–
5.58
).

We also show that
W_{0}
is Fano (
5.58
) and that, up to contracting
E_{2}
instead of
E_{1}
in the factorizations of
f
and
f_{0}
,
\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle W}
blows-up one point in each fiber
\pi_{0}^{-1}(p_{i})
for
i=1,\dotsc,r
(
5.60
),
and
\pi_{0}
is a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle over
\mathbb{P}^{3}
(Lemma
5.61
).

Fano
4
-folds with a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle structure over
\mathbb{P}^{3}
are classified, and in our setting the only possibility is

W_{0}\cong\operatorname{Bl}_{\text{\it pt}}\mathbb{P}^{4}
; this implies that
\widehat{W}=\operatorname{Bl}_{r+1\,\text{\it pts}}\mathbb{P}^{4}
and that
W
is the Fano model of
\operatorname{Bl}_{r+1\,\text{\it pts}}\mathbb{P}^{4}
(Lemma
5.62
).
This, together with the fact that there is a non-trivial contraction of fiber type
W\to Z
, implies that
\rho_{W}\leq 8
and hence
\rho_{X}\leq 9
(Lemma
5.64
), so we get the bound on
\rho_{X}
.

Finally we identify the surface
S\subset W
which is blown-up by
\alpha\colon X\to W
as the transform of a suitable surface
A\subset\mathbb{P}^{4}
, and we get the three possibilities given in the statement.

Proof of Th.
5.1
.

5.4
.

We follow
[
Cas20
, proof of Th. 6.1]
.
We assume that
\rho_{X}\geq 7
, so that
\rho_{Y}\geq 5
. Since
X
is not a product of surfaces,
Th.
1.6
and
1.7
imply that
X
has Leschetz defect
\delta_{X}\leq 2
.

Consider a
K
-negative resolution of
f_{\scriptscriptstyle X}
(see Lemma
2.14
):

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\xi}
\scriptstyle{f_{\scriptscriptstyle X}}
\textstyle{{\widetilde{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{Y.}

Then
Y
can have at most isolated, locally factorial, canonical singularities, contained in the images of the
2
-dimensional fibers of
f
(Th.
3.2
(a)
). Moreover
Y
is log Fano, hence
-K_{Y}
is big, and
-K_{Y}\cdot\operatorname{NE}(g)>0
for every elementary contraction of fiber type
g\colon Y\to Y_{0}
(see
[
Laz04
, Th. 11.4.19]
).

5.5
.

By Th.
3.2
and
[
Cas20
, Lemma 4.10]
, there exists a unique prime divisor
B\subset Y
such that
{f}^{*}B
is reducible, and
{f}^{*}B=E_{1}+E_{2}
with
E_{i}\subset\widetilde{X}
fixed prime divisors of type
(3,2)
(see Th.-Def.
2.16
).

As in the proof of Lemma
3.12
we see that

(E_{1}\cup E_{2})\cap\ell=\emptyset
for every exceptional line
\ell\subset\widetilde{X}
, therefore
E_{1}\cup E_{2}\subset\operatorname{dom}(\xi^{-1})
. We denote by
E_{1}^{\prime},E_{2}^{\prime}\subset X
the transforms of
E_{1},E_{2}\subset\widetilde{X}
, so that
E_{1}^{\prime}\cup E_{2}^{\prime}\subset\operatorname{dom}(\xi)
.

We also note that
B
is a connected component of the discriminant of
f
, and that
B
is smooth outside (possibly) the images of the
2
-dimensional fibers of
{f}
, by Lemma
3.4
.

Lemma 5.6
.

The cone
\operatorname{NE}(f)
has two extremal rays, both of type
(3,2)
, with exceptional divisors
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
. Let
\tilde{\alpha}\colon\widetilde{X}\to\widetilde{W}
be the elementary contraction with exceptional divisor
E_{1}
. We have a diagram:

(5.7)

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\xi}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{{\widetilde{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\xi_{\scriptscriptstyle W}}
\textstyle{{\widetilde{W}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{Y}

where
\alpha\colon X\to W
is a divisorial elementary contraction of type
(3,2)
with
\operatorname{Exc}(\alpha)=E_{1}^{\prime}
, and
\xi_{\scriptscriptstyle W}\colon W\dasharrow\widetilde{W}
is a SQM.
Finally
W
and
\widetilde{W}
are locally factorial and have at most nodes, at the images of some
2
-dimensional fibers of
\alpha
and
\tilde{\alpha}
; moreover
W
is Fano.

We set
S:=\alpha(E_{1}^{\prime})\subset W
. Then
S\subset\operatorname{dom}(\xi_{\scriptscriptstyle W})
, and with a sligh abuse of notation we still denote by
S
its transform in
\widetilde{W}
, which is
\tilde{\alpha}(E_{1})
. Note that
\pi(S)=B
.

Proof.

Since
E_{1}^{\prime}\subset X
is a fixed prime divisor of type
(3,2)
, by Th.-Def.
2.16
there exists
\alpha\colon X\to W
with the properties above, and
W
is Fano. Moreover

E_{1}^{\prime}
does not contain exceptional planes by
[
Cas17
, Rem. 2.17(2)]
, and neither does
E_{1}
, as they are contained in the open subsets where
\xi
is an isomorphism.

We know by Th.
3.2
(d)
that
f
contracts a curve
e_{1}
with
E_{1}\cdot e_{1}<0
, thus
\operatorname{NE}(f)
has an
E_{1}
-negative extremal ray
R
; moreover
R
is
K
-negative, because
f
is. Since
\operatorname{Locus}(R)\subseteq E_{1}
and
E_{1}
does not contain exceptional planes,
R
cannot be small (see Th.
2.8
), thus it is divisorial with
\operatorname{Locus}(R)=E_{1}
. Since
\dim f(E_{1})=2
, also the image of
E_{1}
under the contraction of
R
must be a surface, and

R
is of type
(3,2)
. The same argument holds for
E_{2}
, and the rest of the statement follows from Th.
2.6
.
∎

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
g\colon Y\to Y_{0}
be a birational contraction with
\dim\operatorname{Exc}(g)=1
.
Then
B\cap\operatorname{Exc}(g)=\emptyset
and
\operatorname{Exc}(g)
is the disjoint union of smooth rational curves
C
contained in
Y_{\text{\it reg}}
, with normal bundle
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(-1)^{\oplus 2}
; in particular

\operatorname{NE}(g)\subset K_{Y}^{\perp}
.
Moreover
f^{-1}(C)\cong\mathbb{F}_{1}
, and the
(-1)
-curve
\ell\subset f^{-1}(C)
is an exceptional line in
\widetilde{X}
.

Proof.

The same proof as the one of
[
Cas13
, Lemma 4.5]
applies, with the only difference that, in the notation of
[
Cas13
, Lemma 4.5]
,
\dim\mathcal{N}_{1}(\widetilde{U}/U)
could be bigger than
2
. We take
\tau
to be any extremal ray of
\operatorname{NE}(\widetilde{U}/U)
not contained in
\operatorname{NE}(f_{|\widetilde{U}})
.
Finally we have

B\cap\operatorname{Exc}(g)=\emptyset
because
\ell\cap(E_{1}\cup E_{2})=\emptyset
(see
5.5
).
∎

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
g\colon Y\to Y_{0}
be a divisorial elementary contraction, and set
G:=\operatorname{Exc}(g)
. Then

g
is the blow-up of a smooth point
p\in Y_{0}
, and

D:={f}^{*}G
is a fixed prime divisor, not of type
(3,2)
.

Proof.

Since
g
is elementary and
g(G)
is either a point or an irreducible curve, we have
\dim\mathcal{N}_{1}(g(G),Y_{0})\leq 1
, hence

\dim\mathcal{N}_{1}(G,Y)\leq 2
and
\dim\mathcal{N}_{1}({f}^{-1}(G),\widetilde{X})\leq 4
(see Rem.
2.3
). If there is a component
D
of
{f}^{-1}(G)
which is a fixed prime divisor of type
(3,2)
, let
D_{\scriptscriptstyle X}\subset X
be its transform. Then
\dim\mathcal{N}_{1}(D_{\scriptscriptstyle X},X)=\dim\mathcal{N}_{1}(D,\widetilde{X})\leq 4
by Lemma
2.15
,
while
\delta_{X}\leq 2
and
\rho_{X}\geq 7
(see
5.4
), a contradiction. Thus
G\neq B

(recall that
f^{*}B=E_{1}+E_{2}
with
E_{i}
of type
(3,2)
, see
5.5
) and
D:={f}^{-1}(G)
is a prime divisor; moreover
D
is fixed, because
G
is, and not of type
(3,2)
.

We show that
g
is of type
(2,0)
. By contradiction, suppose that
g
is of type
(2,1)
. As in
[
Cas13
, proof of Lemma 4.6]
we show that there is an open subset
\widetilde{U}\subset\widetilde{X}
such that
D\cap\widetilde{U}
is covered by curves of anticanonical degree
1
. By Lemma
2.13
(c)
,
D_{\scriptscriptstyle X}
still has a nonempty open subset covered by curves of anticanonical degree
1
; this implies that
D_{\scriptscriptstyle X}
and
D
are of type
(3,2)
by
[
Cas17
, Lemma 2.18]
, a contradiction.

Thus
g
is of type
(2,0)
; set
p:=g(G)\in Y_{0}
.
As in
[
Cas13
, proof of Lemma 4.6]

we have a diagram:

(5.10)

\textstyle{{\widetilde{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{{\widehat{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{k}
\textstyle{{\widetilde{X}_{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{1}}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{Y_{0}}

where
h
is a sequence of
D
-negative flips relative to
g\circ f
, and
k
is a divisorial elementary contraction with
\operatorname{Exc}(k)
the transform of
D
, so that
k(\operatorname{Exc}(k))
is contained in the fiber
f_{1}^{-1}(p)
.

We show that
\dim f_{1}^{-1}(p)=1
. If
D
is of type
(3,0)^{\text{\it sm}}
or
(3,1)^{\text{\it sm}}
, this is shown in

[
Cas13
, proof of Lemma 4.6, Step 2]
, so we can assume that
D
is of type
(3,0)^{Q}
. Moreover,
if
G\cap B\neq\emptyset
, the statement is shown in
[
Cas20
, 6.4.3 – 6.4.6]
, thus we can also assume that
G\cap B=\emptyset
, so that
p\not\in g(B)
, and
E_{1}\cap D=\emptyset
.
This implies that
E_{1}\subset\operatorname{dom}(h)
;
let
\widehat{E}_{1}\subset\widehat{X}
be its transform.
Then
\widehat{E}_{1}
is disjoint from
\operatorname{Exc}(k)
; moreover
\widehat{E}_{1}
is covered by curves of anticanonical degree one, therefore it is disjoint from all exceptional lines of
\widehat{X}
(see Lemma
2.13
(c)
). We also have

f_{1}(k(\widehat{E}_{1}))=g(B)
, thus
\widehat{E}_{1}\cap(f_{1}\circ k)^{-1}(p)=\emptyset
.

Suppose by contradiction that
\dim f_{1}^{-1}(p)=2
.
We proceed as in
[
Cas13
, proof of Lemma 4.6, Steps 3 – 6]
, with the difference that in our setting
f_{1}
is not elementary, and
\dim\operatorname{NE}(f_{1}\circ k)=3
.

As in
[
Cas13
]
we see that

f_{1}\circ k
is not
K
-negative,
so there is an extremal ray
R
of
\operatorname{NE}(f_{1}\circ k)
such that
-K_{\widehat{X}}\cdot R\leq 0
; moreover
\operatorname{NE}(f_{1}\circ k)
also contains the extremal ray
\operatorname{NE}(k)
.
We have
\widehat{E}_{1}\cdot R=\widehat{E}_{1}\cdot\operatorname{NE}(k)=0
; on the other hand
\widehat{E}_{1}
is not trivial on the whole cone
\operatorname{NE}(f_{1}\circ k)
,
otherwise
\widehat{E}_{1}
would be the pullback of a divisor from
Y_{0}
, but
(f_{1}\circ k)^{-1}(g(B))
has two irreducible components,
\widehat{E}_{1}
and the transform of
E_{2}
.
Thus
\widehat{E}_{1}^{\perp}\cap\operatorname{NE}(f_{1}\circ k)=R+\operatorname{NE}(k)
, and
\widehat{E}_{1}^{\perp}
also contains the classes of all exceptional lines of
\widehat{X}
. Then we can work within this
2
-dimensional cone and as in
[
Cas13
]
show that
h
is just a
K
-negative flip and
\dim f_{1}^{-1}(p)=1
. This concludes the proof that
\dim f_{1}^{-1}(p)=1
.

Now as in
[
Cas20
, 6.4.7, 6.4.8]
we see that
f_{1}
is
K
-negative, that
Y_{0}
is smooth at
p
, and finally that
g
is just the blow-up of
p
.
∎

Remark 5.11
.

The assumption that
\rho_{X}\geq 7
is essential in the above proof. Consider for instance
S=\operatorname{Bl}_{2\,\text{\it pts}}\mathbb{P}^{2}
,
X=S\times S
,
Y=\mathbb{P}^{1}\times S
, and
f\colon X\to Y
the natural product map given by a conic bundle
S\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Then
Y
has a divisorial elementary contraction
k\colon Y\to\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{F}_{1}
which is the blow-up of a smooth curve
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\{\text{\it pt}\}
.

5.12
.

Y
is weak Fano.

Indeed since
Y
is log Fano,
\operatorname{NE}(Y)
is closed and every one-dimensional face has the form
\operatorname{NE}(g)
for some elementary contraction
g
of
Y
. By
5.4
and Lemmas
5.8
and
5.9
, we always have
-K_{Y}\cdot\operatorname{NE}(g)\geq 0
, thus
-K_{Y}
is nef and big.

5.13
.

Let
Y\dasharrow Y^{\prime}
be a SQM. Then the composition
X\dasharrow Y^{\prime}
is again a special rational contraction with
\rho_{X}-\rho_{Y^{\prime}}=2
(see
[
Cas13
, Rem. 2.8]
), so all the previous steps apply to
Y^{\prime}
as well. As in
[
Cas13
, p. 622]
, using Lemmas
5.8
and
5.9
one shows that if
E\subset Y
is a fixed prime divisor, then
E
can contain at most finitely many curves of anticanonical degree zero.

5.14
.

Let
\varphi\colon Y\to Z
be the anticanonical map.
If
Y
is not Fano, then
\varphi
is small,
\operatorname{Exc}(\varphi)\subset Y_{\text{\it reg}}
, and
B\cap\operatorname{Exc}(\varphi)=\emptyset
. Moreover if
C
is a
connected component of
\operatorname{Exc}(\varphi)
, then
C\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}

with
\mathcal{N}_{C/Y}\cong\mathcal{O}(-1)^{\oplus 2}
,

f^{-1}(C)\cong\mathbb{F}_{1}
, and the
(-1)
-curve
\ell\subset\mathbb{F}_{1}
is an exceptional line in
\widetilde{X}
.

Indeed by
5.13

\varphi
is generically finite on every fixed prime divisor of
Y
, thus it is small, and the statement follows from Lemma
5.8
.

Lemma 5.15
.

Let
k\colon Y\to Y^{\prime}
be a blow-up of distinct smooth points. Then

Y^{\prime}
is weak Fano and has the same singularities as
Y
, and the
following hold:

(a)

every small elementary contraction of
Y^{\prime}
is
K
-trivial;

(b)

if
Y^{\prime}
is not Fano, then the anticanonical map
\varphi^{\prime}\colon Y^{\prime}\to Z^{\prime}
is small, and
\operatorname{Exc}(\varphi^{\prime})
is contained in
(Y^{\prime})_{\text{\it reg}}
and does not contain any point blown-up by
k
. Moreover if
C
is a
connected component of
\operatorname{Exc}(\varphi^{\prime})
, then
C\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}

with
\mathcal{N}_{C/Y^{\prime}}\cong\mathcal{O}(-1)^{\oplus 2}
;

(c)

every divisorial elementary contraction of
Y^{\prime}
is the blow-up of a smooth point, with exceptional divisor not containing any point blown-up by
k
.

Proof.

We note that if
p\in Y^{\prime}
is a point blown-up by
k
, and
C\subset Y^{\prime}
is an irreducible curve containing
p
, then the transform
\widetilde{C}\subset Y
of
C
satisfies
-K_{Y}\cdot\widetilde{C}\geq 0
and
\operatorname{Exc}(k)\cdot\widetilde{C}\geq 1
, which implies that
-K_{Y^{\prime}}\cdot C\geq 2
. This shows that
Y^{\prime}
is weak Fano. Moreover
Y^{\prime}
cannot have
K
-negative small contractions by Th.
2.7
; this gives
(a)
.

Suppose that
Y^{\prime}
is not Fano. By what precedes, the points blown-up by
k
cannot lie in
\operatorname{Exc}(\varphi^{\prime})
, thus
\operatorname{Exc}(\varphi^{\prime})
is contained in the open subset where
k
is an isomorphism, and
k^{-1}(\operatorname{Exc}(\varphi^{\prime}))\subseteq\operatorname{Exc}(\varphi)
. Hence
(b)
follows from the analogous property of
\varphi
(see
5.14
).

Finally let
g\colon Y^{\prime}\to Y^{\prime}_{0}
be a divisorial elementary contraction. It is shown in
[
Cas13
, p. 623]
that

\operatorname{Exc}(g)\cap k(\operatorname{Exc}(k))=\emptyset
; then
by Lemma
5.9

g
must be the blow-up of a smooth point in
Y_{0}^{\prime}
, and we have
(c)
.
∎

Lemma 5.16
.

Up to flops there exists a blow-up
k\colon Y\to Y_{0}
of
r
distinct smooth points
p_{1},\dotsc,p_{r}\in Y_{0}
such that
\rho_{Y_{0}}\leq 2
, and if
\rho_{Y_{0}}=2
, then
Y_{0}
has two distinct elementary rational contractions of fiber type.

We denote by
G_{i}\subset Y
the exceptional divisor over
p_{i}\in Y_{0}
.

Proof.

As in
[
Cas13
, p. 622]
we consider all divisorial extremal rays of
\operatorname{NE}(Y)
and get a map

k\colon Y\to Y_{0}

which is the blow-up of
r
distinct smooth points. Moreover
Y_{0}
is weak Fano and has the same singularities as
Y
.

Let
\psi\colon Y_{0}\dasharrow Y_{0}^{\prime}
be a flop. The composition
\psi\circ k\colon Y\dasharrow Y_{0}^{\prime}
is a rational contraction (see
[
Cas13
, Rem. 2.8]
), and there is a SQM
Y^{\prime}\dasharrow Y
such that the composition
k^{\prime}\colon Y^{\prime}\to Y_{0}^{\prime}
is regular.
By Lemma
5.15

the
r
points blown-up by
k
lie in
\operatorname{dom}(\psi)
. Thus
k^{\prime}
is again the blow-up of
r
distinct smooth points, and we may replace

k\colon Y\to Y_{0}
with
k^{\prime}\colon Y^{\prime}\to Y_{0}^{\prime}
if needed (see
5.13
). Iterating the reasoning, the same holds for any SQM
Y_{0}\dasharrow Y_{0}^{\prime}
.

Suppose that there is a divisorial elementary rational contraction
\pi\colon Y_{0}\dasharrow Y_{0}^{\prime}
. Up to replacing
Y_{0}
and
Y
with a SQM, we can assume that
\pi
is regular.
Then

\pi
must be the blow-up of a smooth point in
Y_{0}^{\prime}
by Lemma
5.15
(c)
, and we replace
Y_{0}
with
Y_{0}^{\prime}
.

In this way, in a finite number of steps, up to flops and up to increasing the number
r
of blown-up points,
we reduce to the case where
Y_{0}
has no divisorial elementary rational contraction, hence
Y_{0}
has no fixed prime divisors (see
[
Cas13
, Rem. 2.19]
).

Suppose now that there is an elementary rational contraction of fiber type
\pi\colon Y_{0}\dasharrow S
with
\dim S=2
; again up to flops we can assume that
\pi
is regular.
Then
S
cannot have divisorial elementary contractions: indeed if
S\to S_{1}
were such a contraction with exceptional divisor an irreducible curve
C
, then
\pi^{-1}(C)
should be a fixed divisor in
Y_{0}
, against our reductions.
Moreover
S
is smooth (see for instance
[
Ou18
, Lemma 5.5]
) and rational,
hence either
S\cong\mathbb{P}^{2}
, or
S\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

If
S\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
(so that
\rho_{Y_{0}}=3
), let us consider the blow-up of the first point
Y_{1}\to Y_{0}
(recall from
5.4
that
\rho_{Y}\geq 5
). The composition
Y_{1}\to S
is not equidimensional, and by
[
Cas20
, Prop. 2.13]
it factors as

\textstyle{{Y_{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Bl}_{\text{\it pt}}}
\textstyle{{Y_{0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{{S^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{S=\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}}}

where
Y_{1}\dasharrow S^{\prime}
is an elementary rational contraction and

S^{\prime}\to S
is the blow-up of a smooth point, thus

S^{\prime}\cong\operatorname{Bl}_{2\text{\it pts}}\mathbb{P}^{2}
.

Consider a
(-1)
-curve of
S^{\prime}
contracted by
S^{\prime}\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
, and its pullback
G
in
Y_{1}
. This is a fixed prime divisor, and up to
to replacing
Y_{1}
with a SQM, we can assume that
G=\operatorname{Exc}(g)
for some divisorial elementary contraction
g\colon Y_{1}\to Y_{0}^{\prime}
; then
g
must be the blow-up of a smooth point, and there is an elementary rational contraction
Y_{0}^{\prime}\dasharrow\mathbb{F}_{1}
.

\textstyle{{Y_{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{{Y_{0}^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{S^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{F}_{1}}}

Then as before we replace
Y_{1}\to Y_{0}
with
g\colon Y_{1}\to Y_{0}^{\prime}
; now using the elementary rational contraction
Y_{0}\dasharrow\mathbb{F}_{1}
,
we blow-down one more point and get
Y_{0}^{\prime\prime}
with
\rho_{Y_{0}^{\prime\prime}}=2
.

In the end the only possible non-small elementary rational contractions of
Y_{0}
are
Y_{0}\to\{pt\}
,
Y_{0}\dasharrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
, or
Y_{0}\dasharrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
. Since
Y_{0}
is a Mori dream space, it has at least
\rho_{Y_{0}}
non-small elementary rational contractions; we conclude that

\rho_{Y_{0}}\leq 2
and we get the statement.
∎

5.17
.

Since
\rho_{Y}\geq 5
(see
5.4
) and
-K_{Y}
is nef and big (see
5.12
), we have
r=\rho_{Y}-\rho_{Y_{0}}\geq 3
and

0<-K_{Y}^{3}=-K^{3}_{Y_{0}}-8r
,
thus
-K^{3}_{Y_{0}}>8r\geq 24
.

Lemma 5.18
.

If
\rho_{Y_{0}}=2
and
Y_{0}
is Fano, then
Y_{0}
is smooth and rational; more precisely either
Y_{0}\cong\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(T_{\mathbb{P}^{2}})
, or
Y_{0}
is isomorphic to a divisor of degree
(1,2)
in
\mathbb{P}^{2}\times\mathbb{P}^{2}
.

Proof.

Since
Y_{0}
is Fano, Lemma
5.15
(a)
implies that
Y_{0}
has no small contraction.
By Lemma
5.16

Y_{0}
has two distinct elementary rational contractions of fiber type, hence they must be regular. By

5.17
we can apply Lemma
4.2
to
Y_{0}
; either we get the statement, or

Y_{0}\cong\mathbb{P}^{2}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
. However the blow-up of
\mathbb{P}^{2}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
at a point has a
divisorial elementary contraction of type
(2,1)
, which is excluded by Lemma
5.15
(c)
.
∎

We recall that
\varphi\colon Y\to Z
is the anticanonical map of
Y
.

Lemma 5.19
.

We have
\rho_{Z}=1
.

Proof.

We have

\rho_{Y}-\rho_{Z}=\dim\operatorname{NE}(\varphi)=\dim(\operatorname{NE}(Y)\cap K_{Y}^{\perp})
, thus
we have
to exhibit
\rho_{Y}-1
curves in
Y
, with anticanonical degree zero, whose classes
in
\mathcal{N}_{1}(Y)
are linearly independent.

Consider one of the points
p_{i}\in Y_{0}
blown-up by
k
, and set
Y_{i}:=\operatorname{Bl}_{p_{i}}Y_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sigma}}{{\to}}Y_{0}
. Let
G\subset Y_{i}
be the exceptional divisor, and consider an extremal ray
R_{i}
of
\operatorname{NE}(Y_{i})
such that
G\cdot R_{i}>0
. Then the contraction of
R_{i}
must be finite on
G\cong\mathbb{P}^{2}
, and has fibers of dimension at most one. By Lemma
5.15
(c)
we conclude that the contraction of
R_{i}
is either small, or of fiber type.

In the small case, let
\Gamma_{i}^{\prime}\subset Y_{i}
be a curve with class in
R_{i}
, and
\Gamma_{i}\subset Y
its transform. Then
\Gamma_{i}^{\prime}
is contained in the open subset here
Y\to Y_{i}
is an isomorphism, hence

K_{Y}\cdot\Gamma_{i}=K_{Y_{i}}\cdot\Gamma^{\prime}_{i}=0
,
\Gamma_{i}\cdot G_{i}>0
, and
\Gamma_{i}\cdot G_{j}=0
for every
j=1,\dotsc,r
,
j\neq i
.

Suppose that the contraction
\psi\colon Y_{i}\to S
of
R_{i}
is of fiber type; note that

\psi(G)=S
, thus
\rho_{S}=1
,
\rho_{Y_{i}}=2
, and

\rho_{Y_{0}}=1
. Moreover
\psi
is
K
-negative and a conic bundle, see Prop.
2.12
.
Let us consider another point
p_{j}\in Y_{i}
blown-up by
k
,
j\neq i
, and note that
p_{j}\not\in G
. Since every irreducible curve of
Y_{i}
containing
p_{j}
must have anticanonical degree
\geq 2
, the fiber
F_{j}
of
\psi
containing
p_{j}
is a smooth rational curve with
-K_{Y_{i}}\cdot F_{j}=2
, and its transform
\Gamma_{ij}\subset Y
has
K_{Y}\cdot\Gamma_{ij}=0
,
G_{i}\cdot\Gamma_{ij}>0
,
G_{j}\cdot\Gamma_{ij}>0
, and
G_{h}\cdot\Gamma_{ij}=0
for every
h\in\{1,\dotsc,r\}\smallsetminus\{i,j\}
.

If
R_{i}
is small for every
i=1,\dotsc,r
, we get
\Gamma_{1},\dotsc,\Gamma_{r}\subset Y
with linearly independent classes in
K_{Y}^{\perp}
. If
\rho_{Y_{0}}=1
, then
r=\rho_{Y}-1
and we are done. If
\rho_{Y_{0}}=2
and
Y_{0}
is not Fano, let
\Gamma_{0}\subset Y
be the transform of an irreducible curve of anticanonical degree zero in
Y_{0}
. Then
G_{i}\cdot\Gamma_{0}=0
for every
i=1,\dotsc,r
(see Lemma
5.15
(b)
), so that
\Gamma_{0},\Gamma_{1},\dotsc,\Gamma_{r}\subset Y
yield again
\rho_{Y}-1
linearly independent classes in
K_{Y}^{\perp}
.

If
\rho_{Y_{0}}=2
and
Y_{0}
is Fano, by Lemma
5.18
we know that
Y_{0}
has two conic bundle structures. Let
\Gamma_{1}^{\prime},\Gamma_{1}^{\prime\prime}\subset Y
be the transforms of the fibers through
p_{1}
of the two conic bundles; as before these fibers must be smooth. Then
K_{Y}\cdot\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}=K_{Y}\cdot\Gamma_{1}^{\prime\prime}=G_{i}\cdot\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}=G_{i}\cdot\Gamma_{1}^{\prime\prime}=0
for every
i=2,\dotsc,r
. Moreover the classes
[\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}],[\Gamma_{1}^{\prime\prime}]
are linearly independent, because their pushforwards in
\mathcal{N}_{1}(Y_{0})
are. Thus we have the
\rho_{Y}-1
curves

\Gamma_{1}^{\prime},\Gamma_{1}^{\prime\prime},\Gamma_{2},\dotsc,\Gamma_{r}
, and we are done.

Suppose now that
R_{1}
is not small; in particular
\rho_{Y_{0}}=1
and
r=\rho_{Y}-1
. We get curves
\Gamma_{12},\dotsc,\Gamma_{1r}
in
Y
with
K_{Y}\cdot\Gamma_{1j}=0
,
G_{1}\cdot\Gamma_{1j}>0
,
G_{j}\cdot\Gamma_{1j}>0
, and
G_{i}\cdot\Gamma_{1j}=0
for every
i,j=2,\dotsc,r
,
i\neq j
.

Recall that
r\geq 3
(see
5.17
). If
R_{2}
is small, then we get a curve
\Gamma_{2}\subset Y
such that
K_{Y}\cdot\Gamma_{2}=0
,
G_{2}\cdot\Gamma_{2}>0
, and
G_{i}\cdot\Gamma_{2}=0
for every
i\neq 2
; the classes of
\Gamma_{2},\Gamma_{12},\dotsc,\Gamma_{1r}
are linearly independent.

If instead
R_{2}
is not small, we get a curve
\Gamma_{23}\subset Y
with
K_{Y}\cdot\Gamma_{23}=0
,
G_{2}\cdot\Gamma_{23}>0
,
G_{3}\cdot\Gamma_{23}>0
, and
G_{i}\cdot\Gamma_{23}=0
for every
i\in\{1,4,\dotsc,r\}
. Then one can check that the classes of
\Gamma_{23},\Gamma_{12},\dotsc,\Gamma_{1r}
are again linearly independent. Indeed given a relation
a\Gamma_{23}+b\Gamma_{12}+c\Gamma_{13}\equiv 0
, intersecting with
G_{i}
for
i=1,2,3
and analysing the signs of
a,b,c
, one gets
a=b=c=0
.
∎

Lemma 5.20
.

Set
B_{0}:=k(B)\subset Y_{0}
. Then
p_{1},\dotsc,p_{r}\in B_{0}
, and for some
\lambda\in\mathbb{Q}_{>0}
we have

-K_{Y}=\lambda B
and
-K_{Y_{0}}=\lambda B_{0}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.19
we have

\dim\operatorname{NE}(\varphi)=\rho_{Y}-1
. On the other hand
B
is disjoint from
\operatorname{Exc}(\varphi)
by
5.14
, thus
B^{\perp}\supset\operatorname{NE}(\varphi)
,

B^{\perp}=K_{Y}^{\perp}
, and finally
-K_{Y}=\lambda B
for some
\lambda\in\mathbb{Q}_{>0}
. This also implies that

-K_{Y_{0}}=\lambda B_{0}
in
Y_{0}
.

Let
i\in\{1,\dotsc,r\}
and let
\Gamma\subset G_{i}
be a curve. Then
-K_{Y}\cdot\Gamma>0
, thus
B\cdot\Gamma>0
and
p_{i}\in B_{0}
.
∎

Lemma 5.21
.

There is a bijection between the set of
exceptional lines in
\widetilde{X}
, and the set of
curves of anticanonical degree zero in
Y
, via
\ell\mapsto f(\ell)
.

Proof.

Let
\ell\subset\widetilde{X}
be an exceptional line, and note that
f(\ell)
is a curve, because
K_{\widetilde{X}}\cdot\ell=1
while
f
is
K
-negative (see
5.4
). We have
\ell\cap(E_{1}\cup E_{2})=\emptyset
(see
5.5
), thus
f(\ell)\cap B=\emptyset
, and
B\cdot f(\ell)=0
. By Lemma
5.20
this is equivalent to
-K_{Y}\cdot f(\ell)=0
. The converse is given by
5.14
.
∎

5.22
.

The composition
\varphi\circ f_{\scriptscriptstyle X}\colon X\dasharrow Z
is a regular contraction,
and it factors through
\alpha\colon X\to W
(see Lemma
5.6
).

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\xi}
\scriptstyle{f_{\scriptscriptstyle X}}
\textstyle{{\widetilde{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{\alpha}}
\textstyle{Z}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\textstyle{W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\xi_{\scriptscriptstyle W}}
\textstyle{{\widetilde{W}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}

Indeed, by Lemma
5.21
,
\varphi\circ f\colon\widetilde{X}\to Z
contracts all exceptional lines of
\widetilde{X}
, therefore
\varphi\circ f\circ\xi=\varphi\circ f_{\scriptscriptstyle X}
is regular (see Lemma
2.13
(a)
).
Similarly, by Lemma
5.6
the indeterminacy locus
\widetilde{W}\smallsetminus\operatorname{dom}(\xi_{\scriptscriptstyle W}^{-1})
is isomorphic, via
\tilde{\alpha}
, to
\widetilde{X}\smallsetminus\operatorname{dom}(\xi^{-1})
, and
\varphi\circ\pi\colon\widetilde{W}\to Z
contracts to points this locus, therefore
\varphi\circ\pi\circ\xi_{\scriptscriptstyle W}\colon W\to Z
is regular.

Lemma 5.23
.

The discriminant of
f
is
B
, and
f
has no intrinsic discriminant (see
3.3
).

Proof.

Suppose by contradiction that the discriminant of
f
has an irreducible component
\Delta_{0}
besides
B
. Then
\Delta_{0}\cap B=\emptyset
(see
5.5
),
thus
B\cdot C=0
for every curve
C\subset\Delta_{0}
. By Lemma

5.20
this gives
K_{Y}\cdot C=0
for every curve
C\subset\Delta_{0}
, namely

\Delta_{0}
is contained in
\operatorname{Exc}(\varphi)
, but
\varphi
is small (see
5.14
).
∎

5.24
.

Let
y_{0}\in Y
be a singular point and
F:=f^{-1}(y_{0})
. Then
y_{0}
is a node,
y_{0}\not\in B
,
\dim\mathcal{N}_{1}(F,\widetilde{X})=1
, and
F\cong\mathbb{P}^{2}\bullet\mathbb{P}^{2}
.

This follows from Th.
3.7
and Lemma
5.23
, because
the fiber
F
is
2
-dimensional (see
5.4
), and
\Delta_{\text{\it intr}}=\emptyset
.

Lemma 5.25
.

If
Y
is singular, then it is not rational.

Proof.

Set

\operatorname{Sing}(Y):=\{y_{1},\dotsc,y_{m}\}
with
m\geq 1
, and

F_{i}:=f^{-1}(y_{i})
.
By
5.24

y_{i}
is a node,
y_{i}\not\in B
,
\dim\mathcal{N}_{1}(F_{i},\widetilde{X})=1
, and
F_{i}\cong\mathbb{P}^{2}\bullet\mathbb{P}^{2}
, for
i=1,\dotsc,m
.

Let
z_{1},\dotsc,z_{r}\in Y
be the smooth points such that
\dim f^{-1}(z_{i})=2
.
Then
f
is a conic bundle over
U:=Y\smallsetminus\{y_{1},\dotsc,y_{m},z_{1},\dotsc,z_{r}\}
, with discriminant
B\cap U
(Lemma
5.23
).
Let us consider the factorization of
f
in elementary steps as in Lemma
5.6
:

\textstyle{{\widetilde{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{{\widetilde{W}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{{Y}}

and recall that
\tilde{\alpha}
is an elementary contraction of type
(3,2)
with exceptional divisor
E_{1}
, and
\widetilde{W}
is locally factorial.
Then

\pi
is smooth with fiber
\mathbb{P}^{1}
over
U
.

Since
y_{1}\not\in B
and
f(E_{1})=B
, we have
\pi^{-1}(y_{1})\cong F_{1}\cong\mathbb{P}^{2}\bullet\mathbb{P}^{2}
. Write

\pi^{-1}(y_{1})=L_{1}\cup L_{2}\subset\widetilde{W}
with
L_{i}\cong\mathbb{P}^{2}
, and let
C_{L_{i}}
be a line in
L_{i}
.
We have
\mathcal{N}_{1}(\pi^{-1}(y_{1}),\widetilde{W})=\tilde{\alpha}_{*}(\mathcal{N}_{1}(F_{1},\widetilde{X}))
, hence

\dim\mathcal{N}_{1}(\pi^{-1}(y_{1}),\widetilde{W})=1
. Moreover
-K_{\widetilde{W}}\cdot C_{L_{1}}=-K_{\widetilde{W}}\cdot C_{L_{2}}=1
, and we conclude that
C_{L_{1}}\equiv C_{L_{2}}
.

If
F_{0}\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}
is a general fiber of
\pi
, then
F_{0}\equiv C_{L_{1}}+C_{L_{2}}\equiv 2C_{L_{1}}
; in particular no divisor in
\widetilde{W}
can have intersection
1
with
F_{0}
, and

\pi_{|\pi^{-1}(U)}\colon\pi^{-1}(U)\to U
cannot be the projectivization of a vector bundle on
U
. Therefore
\pi_{|\pi^{-1}(U)}
defines a nonzero class in the (cohomological) Brauer group
\operatorname{Br}(U)
(see for instance

[
Deb24
, §6.2, 6.3]
).

The following argument is from
[
Deb24
, proof of Th. 6.7]
.
Let
\widehat{Y}\to Y
be the resolution of the nodes; then
U\subset\widehat{Y}
and
E:=\widehat{Y}\smallsetminus U
is a disjoint union of
r
points and
m
smooth quadric surfaces. If
H^{2}(\widehat{Y},\mathcal{O}_{\widehat{Y}})\neq 0
then
\widehat{Y}
and
Y
are not rational, thus we can assume that
H^{2}(\widehat{Y},\mathcal{O}_{\widehat{Y}})=0
, therefore
c_{1}\colon\operatorname{Pic}(\widehat{Y})\to H^{2}(\widehat{Y},\mathbb{Z})
is surjective.

The Thom-Gysin exact sequence

H^{2}(\widehat{Y},\mathbb{Z})\longrightarrow H^{2}(U,\mathbb{Z})\longrightarrow H^{1}(E,\mathbb{Z})=0

yields that the restriction
H^{2}(\widehat{Y},\mathbb{Z})\to H^{2}(U,\mathbb{Z})
is surjective, hence the composition
\operatorname{Pic}(\widehat{Y})\to H^{2}(U,\mathbb{Z})
is surjective, and also
c_{1}\colon\operatorname{Pic}(U)\to H^{2}(U,\mathbb{Z})
is. This implies that

\operatorname{Br}(U)\cong\operatorname{Tors}H^{3}(U,\mathbb{Z})

[
Deb24
, Prop. 6.4]
.

Again using the Thom-Gysin exact sequence

0=H^{1}(E,\mathbb{Z})\longrightarrow H^{3}(\widehat{Y},\mathbb{Z})\longrightarrow H^{3}(U,\mathbb{Z})\longrightarrow H^{2}(E,\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2m}

we find that
\operatorname{Tors}H^{3}(\widehat{Y},\mathbb{Z})\cong\operatorname{Tors}H^{3}(U,Z)
is nonzero. Then
\widehat{Y}
and
Y
are not rational
[
Deb24
, Prop. 6.1]
.
∎

Lemma 5.26
.

The
3
-folds
Y
and
Y_{0}
are smooth and rational, and up to flops,
Y_{0}
belongs to the following list.

\begin{array}[]{||c|c|c|c|c||}\hline\cr\hline\cr\text{N.}&Y_{0}&-K_{Y_{0}}^{3}&\rho_{Y_{0}}&\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr 1&\mathbb{P}^{3}&64&1&\text{Fano}\\
\hline\cr 2&\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(T_{\mathbb{P}^{2}})&48&2&\text{Fano}\\
\hline\cr 3&\text{linear section of }\operatorname{Gr}(2,5)\subset\mathbb{P}^{9}&40&1&\text{Fano}\\
\hline\cr 4&\text{\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{jahnkepeternell}{}{}, Th.\penalty\ 3.6(1)]}}&40&2&\text{weak Fano}\\
\hline\cr 5&\text{\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{jahnkepeternell}{}{}, Th.\penalty\ 3.5(3)]}}&32&2&\text{weak Fano}\\
\hline\cr 6&\text{divisor of degree $(1,2)$ in }\mathbb{P}^{2}\times\mathbb{P}^{2}&30&2&\text{Fano}\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr\end{array}

Proof.

If
\rho_{Y_{0}}=2
and
Y_{0}
is Fano, the statement follows from Lemma
5.18
, and we get N. 2 and 6.
If
\rho_{Y_{0}}=2
and
Y_{0}
is smooth and not Fano,
then
Y_{0}
has small anticanonical map by Lemma
5.15
(b)
,
and two elementary rational contractions of fiber type by Lemma
5.16
; moreover
-K_{Y_{0}}^{3}>24
(see
5.17
).
By Prop.
4.11

we see that, up to flops,
Y_{0}
is either

[
JP08
, Th. 3.5(3) or Th. 3.6(1)]
, or
\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(\mathcal{O}\oplus\mathcal{O}(1)^{\oplus 2})
.
However the blow-up of
\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(\mathcal{O}\oplus\mathcal{O}(1)^{\oplus 2})
at a point (not lying on the curve of anticanonical degree zero) has a divisorial elementary contraction of type
(2,1)
, which is excluded by Lemma
5.15
(c)
. Thus we get N. 4 and 5.

Let us assume that either
\rho_{Y_{0}}=1
, or
\rho_{Y_{0}}=2
and
Y_{0}
is singular and not Fano.
Consider the anticanonical map
\varphi_{0}\colon Y_{0}\to Z_{0}
, and note that
Y_{0}
is nodal by
5.24
. By Lemma
5.15
(b)

\varphi_{0}
contracts curves of anticanonical degree zero in
(Y_{0})_{\text{\it reg}}
to nodes in
Z_{0}
, therefore
Z_{0}
is a nodal Gorenstein Fano
3
-fold with
\rho_{Z_{0}}=1
and
-K_{Z_{0}}^{3}=-K_{Y_{0}}^{3}>24
by
5.17
. Moreover if
\operatorname{Cl}(Z_{0})
is the Weil divisor class group, we have
\operatorname{rk}\operatorname{Cl}(Z_{0})=\rho_{Y_{0}}\in\{1,2\}
.

Then we can apply
[
Pro23
, Th. 1.1 and 1.2]
to
Z_{0}
, and conclude that
Z_{0}
is rational. Note that in the notation of
[
Pro23
]
,
Z_{0}
has genus
g:=\frac{1}{2}(-K_{Z_{0}}^{3})+1\geq 14
, and if
Z_{0}
has index two, then it has degree
d:=\frac{1}{8}(-K_{Z_{0}}^{3})\geq 4
.

Hence
Y_{0}
and
Y
are rational too, so they are smooth by Lemma
5.25
, and we conclude that
\rho_{Y_{0}}=1
and
Y_{0}
is a smooth Fano
3
-fold.
Moreover
r=\rho_{Y}-1\geq 4
and
-K_{Y_{0}}^{3}>8r\geq 32
(see
5.17
).
By classification (see
[
IP99
, §12.2]
) there are three possibilities for
Y_{0}
:
\mathbb{P}^{3}
, a section of the Plücker embedding of the Grassmannian
\operatorname{Gr}(2,5)
in
\mathbb{P}^{9}
by a codimension
3
linear subspace, or
a quadric. However
the blow-up of a smooth
3
-dimensional quadric at a point has a divisorial elementary contraction of type
(2,1)
, which is excluded by Lemma
5.15
(c)
. Thus we get N. 1 and 3.
∎

5.27
.

We note that
k\circ f\colon\widetilde{X}\to Y_{0}
is
K
-negative.

Indeed by Lemma
2.13
(b)
it is enough to check that
k\circ f
does not contract any exceptional line
\ell\subset\widetilde{X}
. By Lemma
5.21

f(\ell)\subset Y
is a curve of anticanonical degree zero, thus
f(\ell)\not\subset G_{i}
for every
i=1,\dotsc,r
, and
k(f(\ell))
is a curve.

Lemma 5.28
.

There is a diagram:

(5.29)

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\xi}
\scriptstyle{f_{\scriptscriptstyle X}}
\textstyle{{\widetilde{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\textstyle{{\widehat{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma}
\textstyle{{X_{0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{0}}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{k}
\textstyle{Y_{0}}

where
\psi
is a sequence of
K
-negative flips,
\widehat{X}
is smooth,

\sigma
is birational and divisorial with pairwise disjoint exceptional divisors
D_{1},\dotsc,D_{r}
, and
f_{0}
is a contraction.
Moreover, for each
i=1,\dotsc,r
, the divisor

D_{i}\subset\widehat{X}
is the transform of
f^{*}G_{i}\subset\widetilde{X}
,
\sigma(D_{i})\subset f_{0}^{-1}(p_{i})
, and there are three possitibilities:

\bullet

D_{i}
is of type
(3,0)^{\text{\it sm}}
and is the exceptional divisor of the blow-up of a smooth point of
X_{0}
;

\bullet

D_{i}
is of type
(3,1)^{\text{\it sm}}
and is the exceptional divisor of the blow-up of a smooth curve contained in
(X_{0})_{\text{\it reg}}
;

\bullet

D_{i}
is of type
(3,0)^{Q}
and is contracted to an isolated hypersurface singularity of
X_{0}
, terminal and locally factorial.

In particular
X_{0}
has at most locally factorial, terminal, isolated hypersurface singularities at
\sigma(D_{i})
for
D_{i}
of type
(3,0)^{Q}
.

Proof.

We set
D_{i}:=f^{*}G_{i}\subset\widetilde{X}
, and with a slight abuse of notation we still denote by
D_{i}\subset\widehat{X}
its transform, for
i=1,\dotsc,r
.

By Lemma
5.9

D_{i}
is a fixed prime divisor, not of type
(3,2)
, for every
i=1,\dotsc,r
;
by Th.-Def.
2.16
,
D_{i}
can be of type
(3,0)^{\text{\it sm}}
,
(3,1)^{\text{\it sm}}
, or
(3,0)^{Q}
.
Moreover
D_{1},\dotsc,D_{r}\subset\widetilde{X}
are pairwise disjoint, thus
D_{i}\cdot C_{D_{j}}=0
when
i\neq j
. Using
[
Cas17
, Lemma 5.29(2)]
we see that

\operatorname{Mov}(\widetilde{X})\cap\langle[D_{1}],\dotsc,[D_{r}]\rangle=\{0\}
. Also note that
k(f(D_{i}))=p_{i}
for every
i=1,\dotsc,r
.

By running in
\widetilde{X}
a MMP for
D_{1}+\cdots+D_{r}
, relative to
k\circ f
,
we get a
diagram as (
5.29
),
where

\psi
is a sequence of
D_{i}
-negative flips for some
i
,

X_{0}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial,
\sigma
is birational with exceptional divisors
D_{1},\dotsc,D_{r}
, and
f_{0}
is a contraction.

Since the MMP is relative to
k\circ f
which is
K
-negative (see
5.27
),
\psi
is a sequence of
K
-negative flips. Therefore
\widetilde{X}\smallsetminus\operatorname{dom}(\psi)
is a finite, disjoint union of exceptional planes (see
[
Cas20
, Lemma 4.1(c)]
), each contained in some
D_{i}
.
Since the
D_{i}
’s are disjoint in
\widetilde{X}
, they stay disjoint also in
\widehat{X}
. Then the description from Th.-Def.
2.16
holds for each one of them. Finally the description of the singularities of
X_{0}
follows from
[
Cas17
, Lemma 2.19]
.
∎

Lemma 5.30
.

Let
i\in\{1,\dotsc,r\}
. Every fiber of
f
over
G_{i}
is one-dimensional.

Proof.

Suppose by contradiction that
f
has a
2
-dimensional fiber
F
over
G_{i}
, so that
F\subset D_{i}
. Since
D_{i}=f^{*}G_{i}
, we have
D_{i}\cdot C=0
for every curve
C\subset F
; in particular
F\cap\operatorname{dom}(\psi)\neq\emptyset
, as
\widetilde{X}\smallsetminus\operatorname{dom}(\psi)
is a finite union of exceptional planes
L
such that either
D_{i}\cdot C_{L}<0
, or
D_{i}\cap L=\emptyset
(see the proof of Lemma
5.28
).

Let
\widehat{F}\subset\widehat{X}
be the transform of
F
. Then
\widehat{F}\subset\operatorname{Exc}(\sigma)
, and
\operatorname{Exc}(\sigma)
cannot contain exceptional lines, see
[
Cas17
, Rem. 5.6]
. The indeterminacy locus of
\psi^{-1}
is a finite union of exceptional lines, therefore
\dim(\widehat{F}\cap(\widehat{X}\smallsetminus\operatorname{dom}(\psi^{-1})))\leq 0
. Since
\dim\sigma(\widehat{F})\leq\dim\sigma(\operatorname{Exc}(\sigma))\leq 1
, there is an irreducible curve
C\subset\widehat{F}
such that
C\subset\operatorname{dom}(\psi^{-1})
and
\sigma(C)=\{pt\}
. We get
\operatorname{Exc}(\sigma)\cdot C<0
, and
D_{i}\cdot C^{\prime}<0
where
C^{\prime}\subset F
is the transform of
C
, a contradiction because
D_{i}\cdot C^{\prime}=0
.
∎

5.31
.

We have
B\cap G_{i}\subset B_{\text{\it reg}}
for every
i\in\{1,\dotsc,r\}
.

This follows from
Lemma
5.30
, because
B
is smooth outside (possibly) the images of the
2
-dimensional fibers of
f
(see
5.5
).

Recall that
\widetilde{X}
contains the fixed prime divisors
D_{1},\dotsc,D_{r},E_{1},E_{2}
, and that we have associated curves
C_{D_{i}}\subset D_{i}
,
C_{E_{j}}\subset E_{j}
, see Th.-Def.
2.16
.

Lemma 5.32
.

If
Y_{0}
is Fano, then

X_{0}
is Fano; otherwise
X_{0}
has a SQM which is Fano. Moreover
f_{0}
is
K
-negative.

Proof.

We keep the same notation as in the proof of Lemma
5.28
. Recall from Lemma
2.13
that the indeterminacy locus of
\xi^{-1}
is the finite, disjoint union of all exceptional lines in
\widetilde{X}
. In particular

\widetilde{X}\smallsetminus\operatorname{dom}(\psi)
is disjoint from
\widetilde{X}\smallsetminus\operatorname{dom}(\xi^{-1})
, because exceptional planes and exceptional lines cannot meet (see Lemma
2.13
(c)
). Therefore
\widehat{X}
contains two types of exceptional lines, those contained in
\operatorname{dom}(\psi^{-1})
, and those in the indeterminacy locus of
\psi^{-1}
. Each of these last ones has positive intersection with some
D_{i}
, because
\psi
is a sequence of flips that are
D_{i}
-negative for some
i
.

Suppose first that
Y_{0}
is Fano. Then every curve of anticanonical degree zero in
Y
must meet
\operatorname{Exc}(k)=G_{1}\cup\cdots\cup G_{r}
, and by Lemma
5.21
this means that every exceptional line
\ell\subset\widetilde{X}
must meet some
D_{i}=f^{*}G_{i}
. On the other hand
\ell
cannot be contained in any
D_{i}
, because

k\circ f
is
K
-negative (see
5.27
), thus
D_{i}\cdot\ell>0
for some
i
.
We conclude that for every exceptional line
\ell^{\prime}\subset\widehat{X}
we have
D_{i}\cdot\ell^{\prime}>0
for some
i
.

Consider now
\sigma^{*}(-K_{X_{0}})=-K_{\widehat{X}}+\sum_{i=1}^{r}m_{i}D_{i}
with
m_{i}=2
(respectively
m_{i}=3
) if
D_{i}
is of type
(3,0)^{Q}
or
(3,1)^{\text{\it sm}}
(respectively
(3,0)^{\text{\it sm}}
).
Using that
D_{i}\cdot C_{D_{j}}=0
for every
i\neq j
, proceeding as in
[
Cas13
, proof of Th. 3.15]
one shows that
-K_{\widehat{X}}+\sum_{i}m_{i}D_{i}
is nef and that
(-K_{\widehat{X}}+\sum_{i}m_{i}D_{i})^{\perp}\cap\operatorname{NE}(\widehat{X})=\operatorname{NE}(\sigma)
; this implies that

X_{0}
is Fano.

If instead
Y_{0}
is not Fano, then we have
\rho_{Y_{0}}=2
and
Y_{0}
contains some curves
C_{j}
of anticanonical degree zero,
j=1,\dotsc,s
, with
C_{1}\equiv\cdots\equiv C_{s}
. The transforms
\widetilde{C}_{j}\subset Y
of these curves are precisely the curves of anticanonical degree zero in
Y
that are disjoint from
\operatorname{Exc}(k)
, and in turn by Lemma
5.21
these are images of the exceptional curves
\ell_{j}\subset\widetilde{X}
that are disjoint from
D_{1},\dotsc,D_{r}
.

We also have
\ell_{1}\equiv\cdots\equiv\ell_{s}
. Indeed fix
j\in\{1,\dotsc,s\}
. We have
f_{*}\ell_{1}=\widetilde{C}_{1}\equiv\widetilde{C}_{j}=f_{*}\ell_{j}
and, by Lemma
5.6
,
\ker f_{*}
is generated by the classes
[C_{E_{1}}],[C_{E_{2}}]
with
E_{1}\cdot C_{E_{2}}>0
and
E_{1}\cdot\ell_{1}=E_{1}\cdot\ell_{j}=0
(see
5.5
). Then
\ell_{1}\equiv\ell_{j}+aC_{E_{1}}+bC_{E_{2}}
with
a,b\in\mathbb{Q}
, and intersecting with
K_{\widetilde{X}}
and
E_{1}
we get
a=b=0
.

We note that the
\ell_{j}
’s are contained in
\operatorname{dom}(\psi)
; let us still denote by
\ell_{j}
their images in
\widehat{X}
. These are the unique exceptional lines in
\widehat{X}
that are disjoint from
\operatorname{Exc}(\sigma)
, and they are still numerically equivalent.

Let us show that their class generates an extremal ray
R
of
\operatorname{NE}(\widehat{X})
.
By contradiction, if
[\ell_{1}]
does not belong to an extremal ray, we can write

\ell_{1}\equiv\sum_{\ell\not\equiv\ell_{1}}\lambda_{\ell}\ell+\sum_{i=1}^{r}\mu_{i}C_{D_{i}}+\Gamma^{+}

where
\lambda_{\ell},\mu_{i}\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}
,
\ell
are exceptional lines, and
\Gamma^{+}
is an effective one-cycle (with coefficients in
\mathbb{Q}
) such that
-K_{\widetilde{X}}\cdot\Gamma^{+}\geq 0
and
D_{i}\cdot\Gamma^{+}\geq 0
for every
i=1,\dotsc,r
. Intersecting with
D_{i}
we get
\mu_{i}=\sum_{\ell}\lambda_{\ell}D_{i}\cdot\ell+D_{i}\cdot\Gamma^{+}\geq\sum_{\ell}\lambda_{\ell}
for every
i=1,\dotsc,r
, and intersecting with
K_{\widetilde{X}}
and using that
-K_{\widetilde{X}}\cdot C_{D_{i}}\geq 2
for every
i
we reach a contradiction.

Let
\eta\colon\widehat{X}\dasharrow\widehat{X}^{\prime}
be the flip of
R
. Then the composite map
X\dasharrow\widehat{X}^{\prime}
factors as sequence of
K
-negative flips, each one
D_{i}
-negative for some
i
, and every exceptional line in
\widehat{X}^{\prime}
has positive intersection with some
D_{i}
. We have a diagram:

\textstyle{{\widehat{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\eta}
\textstyle{{\widehat{X}^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma^{\prime}}
\textstyle{{X_{0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{0}}
\textstyle{{X_{0}^{\prime}}}

where
\operatorname{Exc}(\sigma)=D_{1}\cup\cdots\cup D_{r}
is contained in
\operatorname{dom}(\eta)
, so that
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
are locally isomorphic divisorial contractions, and as in the first part of the proof we see that
X_{0}^{\prime}
is Fano. Moreover
\eta_{0}
is the flip of a small extremal ray generated by the class of the exceptional lines
\sigma(\ell_{j})\subset X_{0}
.

We also note that
f_{0}(\sigma(\ell_{j}))=C_{j}
, so that
f_{0}
does not contract any exceptional line, and it is
K
-negative.
∎

Lemma 5.33
.

The contraction
f_{0}
is special,
\rho_{X_{0}}-\rho_{Y_{0}}=2
,
and the fiber
f_{0}^{-1}(p_{i})
is one-dimensional for every
i=1,\dotsc,r
.

Proof.

We have
\rho_{X}-\rho_{X_{0}}=\rho_{Y}-\rho_{Y_{0}}=r
, therefore

\rho_{X_{0}}-\rho_{Y_{0}}=\rho_{X}-\rho_{Y}=2
. Moreover
f_{0}
is special because
Y_{0}
is smooth (Lemma
5.26
) and if
P\subset X_{0}
is a prime divisor with
f_{0}(P)\subsetneq Y_{0}
, then its transform
\widetilde{P}\subset\widetilde{X}
is different from
D_{1},\dotsc,D_{r}
, thus
f(\widetilde{P})\subset Y
is a prime divisor different from
G_{1},\dotsc,G_{r}
, and
f_{0}(P)=k(f(\widetilde{P}))\subset Y_{0}
is a prime divisor.

Finally, locally around
p_{i}
, diagram (
5.29
) is isomorphic to diagram (
5.10
), therefore
\dim f_{0}^{-1}(p_{i})=1
for every
i=1,\dotsc,r
, as shown in the proof of Lemma
5.9
.
∎

5.34
.

Since
f_{0}
is special, it has at most isolated
2
-dimensional fibers;
outside these,
f_{0}
is a conic bundle (see Prop.
2.12
).

The discriminant divisor of
f_{0}
is
B_{0}
, and
(f_{0})^{*}B_{0}
has two irreducible components, which are the transforms of
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
in
X_{0}
. Indeed
f
and
f_{0}
coincide on
Y\smallsetminus\operatorname{Exc}(k)
and
Y_{0}\smallsetminus\{p_{1},\dotsc,p_{r}\}
respectively, thus this follows from
5.5
and Lemma

5.23
.
Similarly, since
B
is smooth outside the images of the
2
-dimensional fibers of
f
, we see that

B_{0}
is smooth outside (possibly)
p_{1},\dotsc,p_{r}
and the images of the
2
-dimensional fibers of
f_{0}
.

Lemma 5.35
.

The cone
\operatorname{NE}(f_{0})
has two extremal rays, both of type
(3,2)
, with exceptional divisors the transforms of
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
.

Proof.

We proceed as in the proof of Lemma
5.6
; in particular we have shown there that
E_{1}
does not contain exceptional planes.

Since
f
and
f_{0}
coincide on the general fibers over
B
and
B_{0}
respectively,

f_{0}
contracts some curve
e_{1}
with
E_{1}^{\prime\prime}\cdot e_{1}<0
, where
E_{1}^{\prime\prime}\subset X_{0}
is
the transform of
E_{1}\subset\widetilde{X}
. Thus
\operatorname{NE}(f_{0})
has an
E_{1}^{\prime\prime}
-negative extremal
ray
R
, and
R
is
K
-negative because
f_{0}
is. It is enough to show that
R
is not small.

Suppose by contradiction that
R
is small.
By Th.
2.9
for every irreducible component
L
of
\operatorname{Locus}(R)
we have
(L,-K_{X_{0}|L})\cong(\mathbb{P}^{2},\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(1))
. Consider the birational map
X\dasharrow X_{0}
and note that the indeterminacy locus of its inverse has dimensione one, so that it cannot contain
L
. By Prop.
2.10
we conclude that
L
is disjoint from this indeterminacy locus; in particular
L\subset(X_{0})_{\text{\it reg}}
and
L
is contained in the open subset where the map
{X}\dasharrow X_{0}
is an isomorphism. Thefore

L
is an exceptional plane by Th.
2.8
, and its transform in
\widetilde{X}
gives an exceptional plane contained in
E_{1}
, a contradiction.
∎

5.36
.

For every
i=1,\dotsc,r
the fiber
f_{0}^{-1}(p_{i})
is isomorphic to a reducible conic, with a component in the transform of
E_{1}
and one in that of
E_{2}
.

Indeed, since
p_{i}\in B_{0}
(Lemma
5.20
) and
\dim f_{0}^{-1}(p_{i})=1
(Lemma
5.33
),
f_{0}^{-1}(p_{i})
must be isomorphic to a singular conic. On the other hand

f_{0}^{-1}(p_{i})
cannot be a double line, otherwise the class of
(f_{0}^{-1}(p_{i}))_{\text{\it red}}
should belong to both extremal rays of
\operatorname{NE}(f_{0})
.

5.37
.

No
D_{i}
is of type
(3,0)^{\text{\it sm}}
.

Indeed
\sigma(D_{i})\in f_{0}^{-1}(p_{i})
(Lemma
5.28
)
and
f_{0}^{-1}(p_{i})

is isomorphic to a reducible conic by
5.36
,
therefore

\sigma(D_{i})
is contained in an integral curve
\Gamma
of anticanonical degree one.
This is impossible in the case
(3,0)^{\text{\it sm}}
, as the transform of
\Gamma
in
\widetilde{X}
would have anticanonical degree
\leq-2
, contradicting Lemma
2.13
(b)
.

5.38
.

Consider the transform of
E_{j}
in
X_{0}
, for
j\in\{1,2\}
. This is a fixed prime divisor in
X_{0}
, therefore
D_{i}
is adjacent to
E_{j}
in
X
for every
i=1,\dotsc,r
(see p.
2.2
and
[
Cas22
, Lemma 4.4]
). We also note that
B\cap G_{i}\neq\emptyset
in
Y
by Lemma
5.20
, thus
E_{j}\cap D_{i}\neq\emptyset
in
\widetilde{X}
, and the same must be in
X
, because the indeterminacy locus of
\xi^{-1}\colon\widetilde{X}\dasharrow X
has dimension one (Lemma
2.13
(a)
). By Lemma
2.17

we conclude that
E_{j}\cdot C_{D_{i}}=1
if
D_{i}
is of type
(3,0)^{Q}
, while
E_{j}\cdot C_{D_{i}}\in\{0,1\}
if
D_{i}
is of type
(3,1)^{\text{\it sm}}
.

For
i=1,\dotsc,r
we denote by
C_{G_{i}}\subset G_{i}
a line in
G_{i}\cong\mathbb{P}^{2}
.

Lemma 5.39
.

For every
i=1,\dotsc,r
let
x_{i}\in X_{0}
be the singular point of the reducible conic
f_{0}^{-1}(p_{i})
(see
5.36
).
One of the following holds:

(a)

-K_{Y}=2B
,
-K_{Y_{0}}=2B_{0}
,
B_{0}
is smooth at
p_{1},\dotsc,p_{r}
,
X_{0}
is smooth, every
D_{i}
is of type
(3,1)^{\text{\it sm}}
, and
(E_{1}+E_{2})\cdot C_{D_{i}}=1
. Moreover, for every
i
,
\sigma
blows-up the component of
f_{0}^{-1}(p_{i})
contained in
\sigma(E_{j})
, where
j\in\{1,2\}
is such that
E_{j}\cdot C_{D_{i}}=1
;

(b)

-K_{Y}=B
,
-K_{Y_{0}}=B_{0}
,
\operatorname{Sing}(X_{0})=\{x_{1},\dotsc,x_{r}\}
, and every
D_{i}
is of type
(3,0)^{Q}
. Moreover
B\cdot C_{G_{i}}=2
and

B_{0}
has at
p_{i}
a rational double point of type
A_{1}
or
A_{2}
, for every
i=1,\dotsc,r
.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.20
we have
-K_{Y}=\lambda B
and
-K_{Y_{0}}=\lambda B_{0}
for some
\lambda\in\mathbb{Q}_{>0}
.
Then

k^{*}(-K_{Y_{0}})=-K_{Y}+2\sum_{i=1}^{r}G_{i}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptk^{*}(B_{0})=B+\sum_{i=1}^{r}\bigl(B\cdot C_{G_{i}}\bigr)G_{i},

which yields
2=\lambda B\cdot C_{G_{i}}
for every
i=1,\dotsc,r
. Recall also that
\Gamma_{i}:=B\cap G_{i}\subset B_{\text{\it reg}}
for every
i=1,\dotsc,r
(see
5.31
).

Consider
C_{D_{i}}\subset D_{i}\subset\widetilde{X}
. We have
G_{i}\cdot f_{*}(C_{D_{i}})=f^{*}(G_{i})\cdot C_{D_{i}}=D_{i}\cdot C_{D_{i}}=-1
, therefore
f_{*}(C_{D_{i}})=C_{G_{i}}
. Then
B\cdot C_{G_{i}}=B\cdot f_{*}(C_{D_{i}})=f^{*}(B)\cdot C_{D_{i}}=(E_{1}+E_{2})\cdot C_{D_{i}}
.

If
B_{0}
is smooth at some
p_{i_{0}}
, then
B\cdot C_{G_{i}}=1
for every
i
, and
B_{0}
is smooth at every
p_{i}
; moreover
\lambda=2
and
(E_{1}+E_{2})\cdot C_{D_{i}}=1
.
By Rem.
3.9
,

X_{0}
is smooth at
x_{i}
, thus
X_{0}
is smooth along
f_{0}^{-1}(p_{i})
and
D_{i}
must be of type
(3,1)^{\text{\it sm}}
, so we get
(a)
.

Otherwise
B_{0}
is singular at every
p_{i}
, and again by Rem.
3.9

X_{0}
is singular at
x_{i}
, and
D_{i}
must be of type
(3,0)^{Q}
.
Then
E_{j}\cdot C_{D_{i}}=1
for every
j=1,2
and
i=1,\dotsc,r
by

5.38
, hence

B\cdot C_{G_{i}}=(E_{1}+E_{2})\cdot C_{D_{i}}=2
. This also implies that
\lambda=1
,
B\in|-K_{Y}|
, and
B_{0}\in|-K_{Y_{0}}|
.

Moreover
\Gamma_{i}=B\cap G_{i}
is a conic in
G_{i}\cong\mathbb{P}^{2}
,
B
is smooth along
\Gamma_{i}
, and

\Gamma_{i}\cdot_{B}\Gamma_{i}=G_{i}\cdot\Gamma_{i}=-2
. In particular
\Gamma_{i}
cannot be non-reduced, otherwise
4|\Gamma_{i}^{2}
. If
\Gamma_{i}
is smooth, then it is a
(-2)
-curve in
B
, and
p_{i}
is a node for
B_{0}
. If
\Gamma_{i}
is a reducible conic, each component is a
(-2)
-curve for
B
, and
B_{0}
has a singularity of type
A_{2}
at
p_{i}
.
∎

Remark 5.40
.

Let us give a more explicit description of
\psi\colon\widetilde{X}\dasharrow\widehat{X}
and of the divisors
D_{i}
’s. Recall diagram (
5.29
).
In case
(a)
, in
\widehat{X}
we have
D_{i}\cong\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(\mathcal{O}^{\oplus 2}\oplus\mathcal{O}(1))\cong\operatorname{Bl}_{\text{\it line}}\mathbb{P}^{3}
for every
i=1,\dotsc,r
.
Then

\widehat{X}\smallsetminus\operatorname{dom}(\psi^{-1})
is the union of
r
exceptional lines, the transforms of the second components of
f_{0}^{-1}(p_{i})
(contained in
\sigma(E_{j})
such that
E_{j}\cdot C_{D_{i}}=0
). Moreover in
\widetilde{X}
we have
D_{i}\cong\operatorname{Bl}_{\text{\it pt,line}}\mathbb{P}^{3}
,
D_{i}
contains one exceptional plane
L_{i}
in the indeterminacy locus of
\psi
, and
f_{|L_{i}}\colon L_{i}\to G_{i}
is an isomorphism.

In case
(b)
, in
\widehat{X}
each
D_{i}
is isomorphic to a quadric
Q
, smooth or a cone over
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Moreover

\widehat{X}\smallsetminus\operatorname{dom}(\psi^{-1})
is the union of
2r
exceptional lines, the transforms of the components of
f_{0}^{-1}(p_{i})
. Finally in
\widetilde{X}
we have
D_{i}\cong\operatorname{Bl}_{\text{\it 2 pts}}Q\cong\operatorname{Bl}_{\text{\it pt,conic}}\mathbb{P}^{3}
, and
D_{i}
contains two exceptional planes in the indeterminacy locus of
\psi
, both isomorphic to
G_{i}
via
f
. The conic can be smooth or reducible and is isomorphic to
\Gamma_{i}=B\cap G_{i}
.

5.41
.

Let us consider a factorization of
f_{0}
in elementary steps:

\textstyle{{X_{0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{0}}
\scriptstyle{f_{0}}
\textstyle{{W_{0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{0}}
\textstyle{{Y_{0}}}

where
\alpha_{0}
is an elementary contraction of type
(3,2)
with exceptional divisor the transform of
E_{1}
(see Lemma
5.35
), and set
S_{0}:=\alpha_{0}(\operatorname{Exc}(\alpha_{0}))\subset W_{0}
; note that
\pi_{0}(S_{0})=B_{0}
.

Let
T\subset Y_{0}
be the finite set given by the images of the
2
-dimensional fibers of
\pi_{0}
; set
U_{Y}:=Y_{0}\smallsetminus T
and
U_{W}:=\pi_{0}^{-1}(U_{Y})
.

5.42
.

We have
k(\operatorname{Exc}(\varphi))\subset U_{Y}
.

Indeed if
y\in T
, then
\dim\pi_{0}^{-1}(y)=2
, hence
\dim f_{0}^{-1}(y)=2
. In particular
y\neq p_{i}
for every
i=1,\dotsc,r
(Lemma
5.33
),
y^{\prime}:=k^{-1}(y)\in Y
is a point, and
f^{-1}(y^{\prime})\cong f_{0}^{-1}(y)
, thus
\dim f^{-1}(y^{\prime})=2
. Therefore we have
y^{\prime}\not\in\operatorname{Exc}(\varphi)
by
5.14
, and
y\not\in k(\operatorname{Exc}(\varphi))
.

Set
F_{i}:=\pi_{0}^{-1}(p_{i})\subset W_{0}
for
i=1,\dotsc,r
.

Lemma 5.43
.

The
4
-fold
W_{0}
is locally factorial and has at most nodes as singularities,

U_{W}
is smooth, and
\pi_{0|U_{W}}\colon U_{W}\to U_{Y}

is a smooth morphism with fiber
\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Moreover

p_{i}\in U_{Y}
and
F_{i}\subset U_{W}
for every
i=1,\dotsc,r
.

Proof.

First of all we note that
\dim F_{i}=1
because
f_{0}^{-1}(p_{i})=\alpha_{0}^{-1}(F_{i})
has dimension
1
by Lemma
5.33
, therefore
F_{i}\subset U_{W}
.

Every fiber of
\pi_{0}
over
U_{Y}
is an integral rational curve. Indeed if
y\in U_{Y}\smallsetminus B_{0}
, then
\pi_{0}^{-1}(y)\cong f_{0}^{-1}(y)\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}
. If
y\in U_{Y}\cap B_{0}
, then
f_{0}^{-1}(y)
is a reducible conic with one irreducible component in the transform of
E_{1}
, which is contracted to a point by
\alpha_{0}
; thus
\pi_{0}^{-1}(y)
is again irreducible. Then
\pi_{0|U_{W}}

is smooth by
[
Kol96
, Th. II.2.8]
, and since
U_{Y}
is smooth (see Lemma
5.26
),
U_{W}
is smooth.

Finally, since
\alpha_{0}(\operatorname{Sing}(X_{0}))\subseteq\{\alpha_{0}(x_{1}),\dotsc,\alpha_{0}(x_{r})\}
(Lemma
5.39
), outside these points
W_{0}
is locally factorial and has at most nodes, at the images of
2
-dimensional fibers of
\alpha_{0}
(see Th.
2.6
). On the other hand
\alpha_{0}(x_{i})\in F_{i}\subset U_{W}
, thus
W_{0}
is smooth at
\alpha_{0}(x_{i})
for every
i=1,\dotsc,r
.
∎

Set
w_{i}:=\alpha_{0}(x_{i})
for
i=1,\dotsc,r
.

5.44
.

For every
i=1,\dotsc,r
we have
F_{i}\cap S_{0}=w_{i}
,
\pi_{0}(w_{i})=p_{i}
, and

\pi_{0|S_{0}}\colon S_{0}\to B_{0}
is birational and an isomorphism around
w_{i}
.

Indeed clearly
w_{i}\in F_{i}
, and
w_{i}\in S_{0}
because
x_{i}\in\operatorname{Exc}(\alpha_{0})
(see
5.36
). For any one-dimensional fiber
F
of
\pi_{0}
over
B_{0}
, not contained in
S_{0}
, let
\widetilde{F}\subset X_{0}
be its transform. We have
-K_{X_{0}}\cdot\widetilde{F}>0
because
f_{0}
is
K
-negative (see Lemma
5.32
),
\operatorname{Exc}(\alpha_{0})\cdot\widetilde{F}>0
because
F\cap S_{0}\neq\emptyset
, and
-K_{X_{0}}\cdot\widetilde{F}=-K_{W_{0}}\cdot F-\operatorname{Exc}(\alpha_{0})\cdot\widetilde{F}=2-\operatorname{Exc}(\alpha_{0})\cdot\widetilde{F}
, thus

\operatorname{Exc}(\alpha_{0})\cdot\widetilde{F}=1
. Then
\pi_{0|S_{0}}\colon S_{0}\to B_{0}
is birational and is an isomorphism around the point
F\cap S_{0}
.
Moreover note that

F_{i}\not\subset S_{0}
, otherwise
f_{0}^{-1}(p_{i})=\alpha_{0}^{-1}(F_{i})
would have dimension
2
, contradicting Lemma
5.33
.

5.45
.

Recall cases
(a)
and
(b)
from Lemma
5.39
.
In case
(a)
,
S_{0}
is smooth at
w_{1},\dotsc,w_{r}
.
In case
(b)
,
S_{0}
has rational double points of type
A_{1}
or
A_{2}
at
w_{1},\dotsc,w_{r}
.

This follows immediately from
5.44
and Lemma
5.39
.

We recall from Lemma
5.6
that
\alpha\colon X\to W

(respectively
\tilde{\alpha}\colon\widetilde{X}\to\widetilde{W}
) is an elementary contraction of type
(3,2)
with exceptional divisor
E_{1}^{\prime}
(respectively
E_{1}
).

Lemma 5.46
.

The birational map
\alpha_{0}\circ\sigma\circ\psi\circ(\tilde{\alpha})^{-1}\colon\widetilde{W}\dasharrow W_{0}

factors as a SQM
\psi_{\scriptscriptstyle W}\colon\widetilde{W}\dasharrow\widehat{W}
followed by a divisorial contraction
\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle W}\colon\widehat{W}\to W_{0}
, with exceptional divisors the transforms
D_{{\scriptscriptstyle W},i}
of
D_{i}
, and
D_{{\scriptscriptstyle W},i}\subset\widehat{W}_{\text{\it reg}}
, for every
i=1,\dotsc,r
.

(5.47)

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\xi}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{{\widetilde{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\textstyle{{\widehat{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma}
\textstyle{{X_{0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{0}}
\scriptstyle{f_{0}}
\textstyle{W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\xi_{\scriptscriptstyle W}}
\scriptstyle{\zeta}
\textstyle{{\widetilde{W}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{\scriptscriptstyle W}}
\textstyle{{\widehat{W}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle W}}
\textstyle{{W_{0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{0}}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{k}
\textstyle{{Y_{0}}}

Recall cases
(a)
and
(b)
from Lemma
5.39
.

In case
(a)
every
D_{{\scriptscriptstyle W},i}
can be of type either
(3,0)^{\text{\it sm}}
with
\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle W}(D_{{\scriptscriptstyle W},i})=w_{i}=F_{i}\cap S_{0}
(if
E_{1}\cdot C_{D_{i}}=1
; in this case
\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle W}
blows-up
w_{i}
), or
(3,1)^{\text{\it sm}}
with
\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle W}(D_{W,i})=F_{i}=\pi_{0}^{-1}(p_{i})
(if
E_{1}\cdot C_{D_{i}}=0
; in this case
\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle W}
blows-up
F_{i}
), and the two cases are interchanged by contracting
E_{2}
instead of
E_{1}
in the factorization of
f_{0}
as in
5.41
.

In case
(b)
every
D_{{\scriptscriptstyle W},i}
is of type
(3,0)^{\text{\it sm}}
, with
\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle W}(D_{{\scriptscriptstyle W},i})=w_{i}=F_{i}\cap S_{0}
, thus
\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle W}
blows-up
w_{1},\dotsc,w_{r}
.

Proof.

By
5.38

D_{i}
is adjacent to
E_{j}
for every
i=1,\dotsc,r
and
j=1,2
, and if
D_{i}
is of type
(3,1)^{\text{\it sm}}
, we have

E_{j}\cdot C_{D_{i}}\in\{0,1\}
. Note that
\alpha_{0}\circ\sigma\colon\widehat{X}\to W_{0}
contracts first
D_{1},\dotsc,D_{r}
and then
E_{1}
.

Suppose that we are in case
(a)
. Then by Lemma
5.39
every
D_{i}
is of type
(3,1)^{\text{\it sm}}
,
and
either
E_{1}\cdot C_{D_{i}}=1
and
E_{2}\cdot C_{D_{i}}=0
, or viceversa.
In the first case,
E_{1}
and
{D_{i}}
are as in Lemma
2.17
(i)
, in the second as in Lemma
2.17
(ii)
(and conversely for
E_{2}
). Then the statement follows from Lemmas
2.18
and
2.19
, that describe how the divisors
E_{1}
and
D_{i}
intersect and how they can be contracted in different orders.

Recall the geometric description of
D_{i}
and
\psi
given in Rem.
5.40
. If
E_{1}\cdot C_{D_{i}}=1
, then
\tilde{\alpha}(D_{i})\subset\widetilde{W}
is isomorphic to
\operatorname{Bl}_{\text{\it pt}}\mathbb{P}^{3}
, and
still contains an exceptional plane
\tilde{\alpha}(L_{i})
, that lies in the indeterminacy locus of
\psi_{\scriptscriptstyle W}
. Then
D_{W,i}\subset\widehat{W}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{3}
, and is contracted to
w_{i}\in W_{0}
. If instead
E_{1}\cdot C_{D_{i}}=0
, then
\tilde{\alpha}(D_{i})\subset\widetilde{W}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{2}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
, and
is contained in
\operatorname{dom}(\psi_{\scriptscriptstyle W})
, so that

D_{W,i}\subset\widehat{W}
is still isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{2}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
and is contracted to
F_{i}\subset W_{0}
.

Suppose instead that we are in case
(b)
, so that every
D_{i}
is of type
(3,0)^{Q}
. Then
D_{i}
and
E_{j}
are as in Lemma
2.17
(iii)
, and similarly as before the statement follows from Lemma
2.19
.
In this case
\tilde{\alpha}(D_{i})\subset\widetilde{W}
is isomorphic to
\operatorname{Bl}_{\text{\it pt}}\mathbb{P}^{3}
and
contains one exceptional plane, that lies in the indeterminacy locus of
\psi_{\scriptscriptstyle W}
. Then
D_{W,i}\subset\widehat{W}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{3}
and is contracted to
w_{i}\in W_{0}
.
∎

We set
\zeta:=\psi_{\scriptscriptstyle W}\circ\xi_{\scriptscriptstyle W}\colon W\dasharrow\widehat{W}
.
Recall from Lemma
5.6
that
S:=\alpha(E_{1}^{\prime})\subset W
.

Lemma 5.48
.

The
4
-fold
\widehat{W}
is locally factorial, has at most nodes as singularities, and
can contain finitely many pairwise disjoint exceptional lines. If
C\subset\widehat{W}
is an irreducible curve that is not an exceptional line, then
-K_{\widehat{W}}\cdot C\geq 1
, and if
-K_{\widehat{W}}\cdot C=1
, then
C
does not meet any exceptional line.

Moreover the surface
S\subset W
is contained in
\operatorname{dom}(\zeta)
.

With a slight abuse of notation, we still denote by
S
the transform of
S
in
\widehat{W}
; note that
\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle W}(S)=S_{0}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.6
,
W
is Fano, is
locally factorial, and has at most nodes at images of some
2
-dimensional fibers of
\alpha
.

Let
R
be a small extremal ray of
\operatorname{NE}(W)
. By Th.
2.9
for every irreducible component
L
of
\operatorname{Locus}(R)
we have
(L,-K_{W|L})\cong(\mathbb{P}^{2},\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(1))
. Therefore Prop.
2.10
implies that
either
L\cap S=\emptyset
, or
L=S
. This last case
would give
\dim\mathcal{N}_{1}(S,W)=\rho_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}=1
and
\dim\mathcal{N}_{1}(\operatorname{Exc}(\alpha),X)\leq 2
(see Rem.
2.3
), impossible because

\rho_{X}\geq 7
and
\delta_{X}\leq 2
(see
5.4
).
We conclude that
\operatorname{Locus}(R)\cap S=\emptyset
, thus
\operatorname{Locus}(R)\subset W_{\text{\it reg}}
, and
\operatorname{Locus}(R)
is a finite disjoint union of exceptional planes by Th.
2.8
.

Let us consider now the SQM
\zeta\colon W\dasharrow\widehat{W}
.
The same proof of
[
Cas13
, Rem. 3.6]
can be applied here:
since
W
is Fano,
\zeta
can be factored as a sequence of
K
-negative flips; the locus of each flip is contained in the smooth locus, and the loci of the flips are all disjoint. Therefore
\widehat{W}\smallsetminus\operatorname{dom}(\zeta^{-1})
is a finite disjoint union of exceptional lines, contained in
\widehat{W}_{\text{\it reg}}
. If
C\subset\widehat{W}
is an irreducible curve such that
C\cap\operatorname{dom}(\zeta^{-1})\neq\emptyset
, and
\widetilde{C}\subset W
is its transform, we have
-K_{\widehat{W}}\cdot C\geq-K_{W}\cdot\widetilde{C}\geq 1
, and
-K_{\widehat{W}}\cdot C>-K_{W}\cdot\widetilde{C}
if
C
meets some exceptional line. Finally
S\subset\operatorname{dom}(\zeta)
by what precedes.
∎

Lemma 5.49
.

If
Y_{0}
is Fano, then
W_{0}
is Fano; otherwise
W_{0}
has a SQM which is Fano. If
\ell\subset W_{0}
is an exceptional line, then
-K_{Y_{0}}\cdot\pi_{0}(\ell)=0
, and
p_{i}\not\in\pi_{0}(\ell)
for every
i=1,\dotsc,r
. If
C\subset W_{0}
is an irreducible curve that is not an exceptional line, then
-K_{W_{0}}\cdot C\geq 1
.

Proof.

This is similar to the proof of Lemma
5.32
. We keep the same notation as in the proof of Lemma
5.48
. If
Y_{0}
is Fano, then the SQM
\zeta\colon W\dasharrow\widehat{W}
is a sequence of
K
-negative flips, each negative for some
D_{{\scriptscriptstyle W},i}
in
W
. Therefore every exceptional line in
\widehat{W}
has positive intersection with some
D_{W,i}
. Then we consider
\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle W}^{*}(-K_{W_{0}})=-K_{\widehat{W}}+\sum_{i}m_{i}D_{{\scriptscriptstyle W},i}
, where
m_{i}=2
(respectively,
m_{i}=3
) if
D_{{\scriptscriptstyle W},i}
is of type
(3,1)^{\text{\it sm}}
(respectively,
(3,0)^{\text{\it sm}}
), and we show that
-K_{\widehat{W}}+\sum_{i}m_{i}D_{{\scriptscriptstyle W},i}
is nef and that
(-K_{\widehat{W}}+\sum_{i}m_{i}D_{{\scriptscriptstyle W},i})^{\perp}\cap\operatorname{NE}(\widehat{W})=\operatorname{NE}(\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle W})
. This shows that
W_{0}
is Fano.

If instead
Y_{0}
is weak Fano with
\rho_{Y_{0}}=2
, then there is a
K
-positive flip
\eta_{\scriptscriptstyle W}\colon W_{0}\dasharrow W_{0}^{\prime}
such that
W_{0}^{\prime}
is Fano; the indeterminacy locus of
\eta_{\scriptscriptstyle W}
is given by the exceptional lines
\alpha_{0}(\sigma(\ell_{j}))
, in the notation of the proof of Lemma
5.32
. In particular, if
\ell\subset W_{0}
is an exceptional line, then
\ell=\alpha_{0}(\sigma(\ell_{j}))
for some
j
, and
\pi_{0}(\ell)=f_{0}(\sigma(\ell_{j}))=C_{j}
a curve of anticanonical degree zero in
Y_{0}
, so that
p_{i}\not\in\pi_{0}(\ell)
for every
i=1,\dotsc,r
by Lemma
5.15
(b)
.
∎

5.50
.

Recall from Lemma
5.43
that
\pi_{0|U_{W}}\colon U_{W}\to U_{Y}
is a smooth morphism with fiber
\mathbb{P}^{1}
.
Since
Y_{0}
is rational (see Lemma
5.26
), its Brauer group
\operatorname{Br}(Y_{0})
is trivial, and since
Y_{0}\smallsetminus U_{Y}
is finite, we have
\operatorname{Br}(U_{Y})\cong\operatorname{Br}(Y_{0})=0
by purity
[
Gab81
, Th.
2^{\prime}
p. 131]
. We conclude that

U_{W}=\mathbb{P}_{U_{Y}}(\mathcal{E})
for some rank two vector bundle
\mathcal{E}
on
U_{Y}
(see for instance
[
Deb24
, §6.3]
). Moreover
\det\mathcal{E}\in\operatorname{Pic}(U_{Y})
extends to a line bundle
L\in\operatorname{Pic}(Y_{0})
.

Lemma 5.51
.

Let
i\in\{1,\dotsc,r\}
, and suppose that
C\subset Y_{0}
is a smooth rational curve with
-K_{Y_{0}}\cdot C=2
and
p_{i}\in C
. Then
p_{j}\not\in C
for every
j\neq i
, and one of the following holds:

(i)

L\cdot C
is odd,
\pi_{0}^{-1}(C)\cong\mathbb{F}_{1}
,
and
\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle W}
blows-up
F_{i}
;

(ii)

L\cdot C
is even,
\pi_{0}^{-1}(C)\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
, and

\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle W}
blows-up
w_{i}
.

Proof.

Since the transform of
C
in
Y
has anticanonical degree zero, we have
C\subset U_{Y}
by
5.42
; moreover
p_{j}\not\in C
for every
j\neq i
, otherwise
-K_{Y}
would not be nef.

Set
S:=\pi_{0}^{-1}(C)\cong\mathbb{F}_{e}
with
e\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. We
apply Rem.
2.5
and
keep the same notation; in particular
L\cdot C\equiv e\mod 2
. We have
-K_{W_{0}}\cdot\Gamma^{-}=2-e
and
\pi_{0}(\Gamma^{-})=C
,
therefore by Lemma
5.49

\Gamma^{-}
is not an exceptional line and
e\in\{0,1\}
.

Suppose that
\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle W}
blows-up
F_{i}
. Since
F_{i}\cap\Gamma^{-}\neq\emptyset
, and
-K_{W_{0}}\cdot\Gamma^{-}=2-e
, the transform of
\Gamma^{-}
in
\widehat{W}
has anticanonical degree
-e
, which implies that
e=1
(see Lemma
5.48
), and
L\cdot C
is odd.

If instead
\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle W}
blows-up
w_{i}
, we claim that
e\neq 1
, because in that case we would have
-K_{W_{0}}\cdot\Gamma^{-}=1
and
-K_{W_{0}}\cdot\Gamma^{+}=3
.
If
w_{i}\in\Gamma^{-}
, then the transform of
\Gamma^{-}
in
\widehat{W}
has anticanonical degree
-2
, impossible by Lemma
5.48
. Otherwise, we can assume that
w_{i}\in\Gamma^{+}
, and then the transform of
\Gamma^{+}
has degree zero, again impossible.
Hence
e=0
and
L\cdot C
is even.
∎

Lemma 5.52
.

Suppose that
C\subset Y_{0}
is a smooth rational curve with
-K_{Y_{0}}\cdot C=4
and
p_{i},p_{j}\in C
, with
i\neq j
. Then
p_{k}\not\in C
for every
k\neq i,j
, and one of the following holds:

(i)

L\cdot C
is odd
and
\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle W}
blows-up either
F_{i}
and
F_{j}
, or

w_{i}
and
w_{j}
;

(ii)

L\cdot C
is even
and

\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle W}
blows-up either
F_{i}
and
w_{j}
, or
w_{i}
and
F_{j}
.

Proof.

Again, since the transform of
C
in
Y
has anticanonical degree zero, we have
C\subset U_{Y}

by
5.42
, and
p_{k}\not\in C
for every
k\neq i,j
.
Similarly as before we apply Rem.
2.5
; we have
\pi_{0}^{-1}(C)\cong\mathbb{F}_{e}
with
L\cdot C\equiv e\mod 2
,

-K_{W_{0}}\cdot\Gamma^{-}=4-e
, and
-K_{W_{0}}\cdot\Gamma^{+}=4+e
; moreover
\Gamma^{-}
is not an exceptional line by Lemma
5.49
,
therefore
e\in\{0,1,2,3\}
.

If
\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle W}
blows-up
F_{i}
and
F_{j}
, then
\Gamma^{-}
meets both fibers and its transform in
\widehat{W}
has anticanonical degree
-e
, which yields
e=1
(see Lemma
5.48
), and we have
(i)
.

If
\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle W}
blows-up
F_{i}
and
w_{j}
, then the transform of
F_{j}
in
\widehat{W}
is an exceptional line.
We show that
e=0
, which gives
(ii)
. If
e>0
, then
-K_{W_{0}}\cdot\Gamma^{-}\leq 3
; since
\Gamma^{-}\cap F_{i}\neq\emptyset
, if
w_{j}\in\Gamma^{-}
, the transform of
\Gamma^{-}
in
\widehat{W}
would have anticanonical degree
-e-1
, impossible by Lemma
5.48
.
Then

w_{j}\not\in\Gamma^{-}
, and we can assume that

w_{j}\in\Gamma^{+}
, so that the transforms of
\Gamma^{-}
and
\Gamma^{+}
have degrees, respectively,
2-e
and
e-1
, which yields
e=3
. Then the transforms of
\Gamma^{-}
and
F_{j}
are exceptional lines in
\widehat{W}
, but they intersect, which is again impossible.

Finally suppose that
\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle W}
blows-up
w_{i}
and
w_{j}
.
We show that
\Gamma^{-}
cannot contain any of these two points.
Indeed
\Gamma^{-}
can contain at most one of them, say
w_{i}
. Then the transform of
\Gamma^{-}
in
\widehat{W}
has degree
1-e
, which implies
e\in\{0,2\}
(see Lemma
5.48
). If
e=0
, then
S\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
, and there is a section of
\pi_{0|S}
, containing
w_{i}
and
w_{j}
, of degree
4
or
6
, in both cases impossible by Lemma
5.48
, as its transform would have degree
-2
or
0
respectively. If
e=2
, then both the transforms of
\Gamma^{-}
and
F_{j}
are exceptional lines in
\widehat{W}
, but they intersect, which is again impossible by Lemma
5.48
.
Thus
w_{i},w_{j}\not\in\Gamma^{-}
.

Therefore
e>0
and we can choose
\Gamma^{+}
containing both points. Then its transform has degree
e-2
, hence
e\in\{1,3\}
(see Lemma
5.48
), and we have again
(i)
.
∎

We recall that, up to flops,
Y_{0}
belongs to the list of six
3
-folds given in Lemma
5.26
.

Lemma 5.53
.

Suppose that
Y_{0}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(T_{\mathbb{P}^{2}})
or to a linear section of
\operatorname{Gr}(2,5)
.
If there exists a smooth rational curve
C\subset Y_{0}
with
-K_{Y_{0}}\cdot C=4
and
p_{1},p_{2}\in C
, then
L\cdot C
is odd.

Proof.

In both cases there exist
smooth rational curves

\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}\subset Y_{0}
with
\Gamma_{1}\equiv\Gamma_{2}
,
-K_{Y_{0}}\cdot\Gamma_{i}=2
, and
p_{i}\in\Gamma_{i}
, for
i=1,2
. Indeed for
\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(T_{\mathbb{P}^{2}})
we just consider the fibers of one of the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundles onto
\mathbb{P}^{2}
(note that
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
cannot be contained in the same fiber, see Lemma
5.51
). For the linear section of
\operatorname{Gr}(2,5)
, it is well-known that it is covered by an irreducible family of lines in the Plücker embedding
\operatorname{Gr}(2,5)\subset\mathbb{P}^{9}
, see for instance
[
San14
, §2.2]
.

Set
d:=L\cdot\Gamma_{i}
.
By Lemma
5.51
, if
d
is odd, then
\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle W}
blows-up
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
, while if
d
is even, then
\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle W}
blows-up
w_{1}
and
w_{2}
. Thus
L\cdot C
is odd by Lemma
5.52
.
∎

Lemma 5.54
.

Y_{0}\not\cong\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(T_{\mathbb{P}^{2}})
.

Proof.

Assume by contradiction that
Y_{0}=\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(T_{\mathbb{P}^{2}})
. For
i=1,2

let
\pi_{i}\colon Y_{0}\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
be a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle, and
\Gamma_{i}\subset Y_{0}
a fiber of
\pi_{i}
.

We show that there exists a smooth rational curve
C\subset Y_{0}
with
C\equiv\Gamma_{1}+\Gamma_{2}
and containing
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
.
Note that
\pi_{i}(p_{1})\neq\pi_{i}(p_{2})
for
i=1,2
, otherwise
\operatorname{Bl}_{p_{1},p_{2}}Y_{0}
would not be weak Fano.
We consider the line
\ell:=\overline{\pi_{1}(p_{1})\pi_{1}(p_{2})}\subset\mathbb{P}^{2}
and the surface
S:=\pi_{1}^{-1}(\ell)
.
Then
S\cong\mathbb{F}_{1}
, and
\pi_{2|S}\colon S\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
is a blow-up with exceptional curve

\overline{\Gamma}_{2}\subset S
, with
\overline{\Gamma}_{2}\equiv\Gamma_{2}
.

If
p_{1}\in\overline{\Gamma}_{2}
, then
\overline{\Gamma}_{2}
and the fiber of
\pi_{1}
through
p_{2}
would give two intersecting curves of anticanonical degree zero in
\operatorname{Bl}_{p_{1},p_{2}}Y_{0}
, contradicting Lemma
5.15
(b)
. Thus none of
p_{1},p_{2}
is contained in
\overline{\Gamma}_{2}
, and we can find in
S
a smooth rational curve
C
such that
p_{1},p_{2}\in C
and
C\equiv\Gamma_{1}+\Gamma_{2}
, hence
-K_{Y_{0}}\cdot C=4
.

Set
d_{j}:=L\cdot\Gamma_{j}
for
j=1,2
. Note that every
p_{i}
is contained both in a fiber of
\pi_{1}
and in a fiber of
\pi_{2}
, hence by Lemma
5.51
we have
d_{1}\equiv d_{2}\mod 2
. Then

L\cdot C=d_{1}+d_{2}
is even, but this contradicts Lemma
5.53
.
∎

Lemma 5.55
.

Y_{0}
is not isomorphic to a linear section of
\operatorname{Gr}(2,5)
.

Proof.

By contradiction suppose that
Y_{0}
is isomorphic to a linear section of
\operatorname{Gr}(2,5)\subset\mathbb{P}^{9}
.
The Hilbert scheme of conics in
Y_{0}
is studied in detail in
[
San14
, §2.3]
; in particular it is irreducible, and
Y_{0}
also contains double lines (
[
San14
, Prop. 2.44]
), so that if
C\subset Y_{0}
is a conic,
L\cdot C
must be even.
By
[
San14
, Cor. 2.43]
, there exists a conic
C\subset Y_{0}
containing
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
; we have
-K_{Y_{0}}\cdot C=4
. By Lemma
5.53

C
cannot be smooth.

The two points
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
cannot be contained in the same component of
C
, thus
C=C_{1}\cup C_{2}
with
p_{i}\in C_{i}
,
p_{i}\neq C_{1}\cap C_{2}
, and
-K_{Y_{0}}\cdot C_{i}=2
, for
i=1,2
. Then the transforms of
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
in
\operatorname{Bl}_{p_{1},p_{2}}Y_{0}
are two curves of anticanonical degree zero which intersect, contradicting Lemma
5.15
(b)
.
∎

Lemma 5.56
.

Y_{0}
is not isomorphic to a divisor of degree
(1,2)
in
\mathbb{P}^{2}\times\mathbb{P}^{2}
.

Proof.

Assume by contradiction that
Y_{0}\subset\mathbb{P}^{2}\times\mathbb{P}^{2}
is a divisor of degree
(1,2)
.
Let

\pi_{i}\colon Y_{0}\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
, for
i=1,2
, be the restrictions of the two projections, and

C_{i}\subset Y_{0}
a general fiber of
\pi_{i}
. Note that
\pi_{2}
is a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle, while
\pi_{1}
is a conic bundle, with discriminant a cubic curve in
\mathbb{P}^{2}
. In particular, if
\Gamma_{1}\subset Y_{0}
is a component of a reducible fiber of
\pi_{1}
, then
C_{1}\equiv 2\Gamma_{1}
.

Let
D\subset Y_{0}
be the pullback of the discriminant curve of
\pi_{1}
in
\mathbb{P}^{2}
, so that
D
is covered by rational curves of anticanonical degree one. Note that
D\in|\pi_{1}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(3)|
, thus
D\cdot C_{2}=3
.

We show that also the divisor
\pi_{0}^{*}(D)
in
W_{0}
is covered by curves of anticanonical degree one.
Indeed let
\Gamma\subset D
be a component of a general reducible conic. Since
\Gamma
is general, we have
\Gamma\subset U_{Y}

(recall from
5.41
and Lemma
5.43
that
U_{Y}
is the open subset of
Y
where
\pi_{0}
is smooth, and that
Y\smallsetminus U_{Y}
is finite); consider
S:=\pi_{0}^{-1}(\Gamma)\subset W_{0}
and apply Rem.
2.5
. Note that, since
Y_{0}
is Fano,
W_{0}
is Fano by Lemma
5.49
,
and
-K_{W_{0}}\cdot\Gamma^{-}=1-e
, where
S\cong\mathbb{F}_{e}
. Hence we have
e=0
,
S\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
, and the horizontal curves in
S
have anticanonical degree one; thus

\pi_{0}^{*}(D)
is covered by curves of anticanonical degree one.

There exist fibers
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
, of
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
respectively, containing
p_{1}
; note that
C_{1}
must be a smooth fiber, otherwise
\operatorname{Bl}_{p_{1}}Y_{0}
would not be weak Fano. We have
L\cdot C_{1}=2L\cdot\Gamma_{1}
even; then Lemma
5.51
implies that
\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle W}
blows-up
w_{1}
, and that
L\cdot C_{2}
is even too.

Let us consider now
S^{\prime}:=\pi_{0}^{-1}(C_{2})
.
Since
L\cdot C_{2}
is even, by Lemma
5.51
we have again
S^{\prime}\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
, and the horizontal curves have anticanonical degree
2
in
W_{0}
(see Rem.
2.5
). Let
\widetilde{C}_{2}
be the horizontal curve containing
w_{1}\in S^{\prime}
. Then
\pi_{0}^{*}(D)\cdot\widetilde{C}_{2}=D\cdot C_{2}>0
; on the other hand
w_{1}\not\in\pi_{0}^{*}(D)
(because
w_{1}
cannot be contained in a curve of anticanonical degree one, by Lemma
5.48
), thus
\widetilde{C}_{2}
must intersect
\pi_{0}^{*}(D)
in some point different from
w_{1}
, and
\widetilde{C}_{2}
intersects some curve
\Gamma
with
-K_{W_{0}}\cdot\Gamma=1
.

Then the transform of
\widetilde{C}_{2}
in
\widehat{W}
gives an exceptional line which meets a curve of anticanonical degree one, contradicting Lemma
5.48
.
∎

Lemma 5.57
.

Y_{0}
is Fano.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.26
, if
Y_{0}
is not Fano, then
\rho_{Y_{0}}=2
and up to flops
Y_{0}
is isomorphic to one of the weak Fano
3
-folds in
[
JP08
, Th. 3.5(3), Th. 3.6(1)]
.

We consider first
[
JP08
, Th. 3.6(1)]
, where

Y_{0}=\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(\mathcal{F})
with
\mathcal{F}
a rank
2
vector bundle.
There is a flop
Y_{0}\dasharrow Y_{0}^{\prime}
where
Y_{0}^{\prime}
is
[
JP08
, Th. 3.5(4)]
, namely

Y_{0}^{\prime}\subset P^{\prime}:=\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(\mathcal{O}\oplus\mathcal{O}(1)^{\oplus 3})
is a general divisor in the linear system
|2\eta-F|
,
\eta
the tautological class and
F
a fiber of the
\mathbb{P}^{3}
-bundle
P^{\prime}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
. The
\mathbb{P}^{3}
-bundle restricts to a quadric bundle
Y_{0}^{\prime}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
.
Moreover
P^{\prime}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
has a section
\ell^{\prime}\subset P^{\prime}
with normal bundle
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(-1)^{\oplus 3}
, in fact
\ell^{\prime}
is an exceptional line in
P^{\prime}
, and
