\ell^{\prime}\subset Y_{0}^{\prime}
is the flopping curve. There is a flip
P\dasharrow P^{\prime}
where
P=\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(\mathcal{O}\oplus\mathcal{O}(1)^{\oplus 2})
is a Fano
4
-fold containing
Y_{0}
, and the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle

\pi\colon Y_{0}\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
is given by the restriction of the
\mathbb{P}^{2}
-bundle
P\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
.

The fibers
C\subset Y_{0}
of
\pi
are the transforms of the lines in the fibers of the quadric bundle
Y_{0}^{\prime}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
which intersect
\ell^{\prime}
.
If
\Gamma\subset Y_{0}
is the transform of a general line in a fiber of the quadric bundle, one can check that

\Gamma\equiv C+\ell
, where
\ell\subset Y_{0}
is the flopping curve.

Recall that
p_{i}\not\in\ell
for every
i
(see Lemma
5.15
(b)
), thus
\ell
is contained in the open subset where
k\colon Y\to Y_{0}
is an isomorphism, and
f^{-1}(k^{-1}(\ell))\cong(f_{0})^{-1}(\ell)
(see (
5.29
)). By
5.14
we conclude that
f_{0}^{-1}(\ell)\cong\mathbb{F}_{1}

and
\ell\subset U_{Y}
(see
5.41
and Lemma
5.43
), thus
L\cdot\ell
is odd by Rem.
2.5
.
We denote by
p^{\prime}_{1}
the image of
p_{1}
in
Y_{0}^{\prime}
.
Consider the fiber
F
of the quadric bundle through
p_{1}^{\prime}
, and
let
\overline{\Gamma}^{\prime}\subset F
be a line through
p_{1}^{\prime}
. Then

\overline{\Gamma}^{\prime}\cap\ell^{\prime}=\emptyset
, otherwise in

\operatorname{Bl}_{p^{\prime}_{1}}Y_{0}^{\prime}
these curves would be two intersecting curves of anticanonical degree zero, contradicting Lemma
5.15
(b)
.

Let
\overline{\Gamma}\subset Y_{0}
be the transform of
\overline{\Gamma}^{\prime}
.
Consider now the point
p_{1}\in Y_{0}
and let
\overline{C}
be the fiber of
\pi

containing
p_{1}
.
We have
-K_{Y_{0}}\cdot\overline{\Gamma}=2
,
p_{1}\in\overline{\Gamma}
, and
\overline{\Gamma}\equiv\overline{C}+\ell
. In particular we see that
L\cdot\overline{\Gamma}
and
L\cdot\overline{C}
have different parity, but this contradicts
Lemma
5.51
.

The case where
Y_{0}
is isomorphic to the weak Fano
3
-fold in
[
JP08
, Th. 3.5(3)]
is similar. Now
Y_{0}\subset\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(\mathcal{O}^{\oplus 2}\oplus\mathcal{O}(1)^{\oplus 2})
in the linear system
|2\eta|
, and
Y_{0}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
is a quadric bundle. Moreover, considering the flop
Y_{0}\dasharrow Y_{0}^{\prime}
, we have that
Y_{0}^{\prime}
is of the same type as
Y_{0}
. In this case
Y_{0}
has two flopping curves
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
, with
\ell_{1}\equiv\ell_{2}
, both sections of the quadric bundle (
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
are disjoint for
Y_{0}
general, but possibly
\ell_{1}=\ell_{2}
for some special
Y_{0}
). Similarly as in the previous case, using lines in the fibers of the quadric bundles on
Y_{0}
and
Y_{0}^{\prime}
, we construct two smooth rational curves
C,\Gamma\subset Y_{0}
through
p_{1}
with
\Gamma\equiv C+\ell_{1}
and
-K_{Y_{0}}\cdot\Gamma=-K_{Y_{0}}\cdot C=2
; here
C
is a line in the fiber of the quadric bundle
Y_{0}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
through
p_{1}
, while
\Gamma
is a section. In the end we
obtain a contradiction with the parity of the intersection of
L
with these curves.
∎

5.58
.

We have
Y_{0}\cong\mathbb{P}^{3}
,
Y\cong\operatorname{Bl}_{r\,\text{\it pts}}\mathbb{P}^{3}
, and
W_{0}
is Fano.

Indeed in Lemmas
5.54
–
5.57
we have excluded all the other cases of Lemma
5.26
. Then
W_{0}
is Fano by Lemma
5.49
.

Lemma 5.59
.

Let
\ell\subset\mathbb{P}^{3}
be a line. Then
L\cdot\ell
is odd, and
\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle W}
blows-up either
r
points or
r
fibers.

Proof.

Indeed if
\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle W}
blows-up both points and fibers, since
r\geq 3
we can find three points
p_{i},p_{j},p_{k}
such that
\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle W}
blows-up
w_{i},w_{j},F_{k}
(or conversely
F_{i},F_{j},w_{k}
). Then by Lemma
5.52
we have
L\cdot\overline{p_{i}p_{j}}
odd and
L\cdot\overline{p_{i}p_{k}}
even, a contradiction. Therefore
\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle W}
blows-up either
r
points, or
r
fibers, and
L\cdot\ell
is odd again
by Lemma
5.52
.
∎

5.60
.

Up to switching
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
, from now on we assume that

\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle W}
blows-up
r
points.

Indeed, suppose that
\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle W}
blows-up
r
fibers. This means that every
D_{{\scriptscriptstyle W},i}
is of type
(3,1)^{\text{\it sm}}
, so by Lemma
5.46
we are in case
(a)
and, if we change the factorization of
f_{0}
in
5.41
by contracting
E_{2}
instead of
E_{1}
, in the new factorization every
D_{{\scriptscriptstyle W},i}
is of type
(3,0)^{\text{\it sm}}
, namely
\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle W}
blows-up
r
points.

Lemma 5.61
.

The morphism
\pi_{0}
has no 2-dimensional fibers.

Proof.

Recall that
F_{1}
is a smooth fiber of
\pi_{0}
(see Lemma
5.43
). We consider the blow-up
\sigma_{1}\colon\widehat{W}_{1}\to W_{0}
of the first point
w_{1}\in F_{1}\subset W_{0}
; the transform of
F_{1}
in
\widehat{W}_{1}
is an exceptional line, and
we have a diagram:

\textstyle{{W_{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{1}}
\textstyle{{\widehat{W}_{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{1}}
\textstyle{{W_{0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{0}}
\textstyle{{Y_{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Bl}_{p_{1}}}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{P}^{3}}}

where
\widehat{W}_{1}\dasharrow W_{1}
flips the transform of
F_{1}
, which is contained in
(\widehat{W}_{1})_{\text{\it reg}}
(see Lemma
5.46
).
The exceptional divisor
D_{{\scriptscriptstyle W},1}=\operatorname{Exc}(\sigma_{1})\subset\widehat{W}_{1}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{3}
, and its transform
D_{1}^{\prime}\subset W_{1}
is isomorphic to
\operatorname{Bl}_{\text{\it pt}}\mathbb{P}^{3}
, so that
\pi_{1|D_{1}^{\prime}}\colon D_{1}^{\prime}\to G_{1}
is a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle, where

G_{1}\cong\mathbb{P}^{2}\subset Y_{1}
is the exceptional divisor over
p_{1}
.

Since
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{0}
are isomorphic over
Y_{1}\smallsetminus G_{1}
and
\mathbb{P}^{3}\smallsetminus\{p_{1}\}
respectively, it is enough to show that
\pi_{1}
has no
2
-dimensional fibers.

We have
Y_{1}=\operatorname{Bl}_{p_{1}}\mathbb{P}^{3}
and there is a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle
\beta\colon Y_{1}\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
. Moreover
Y_{1}
is Fano, thus
W_{1}
is Fano too, because if
W_{1}
contained an exceptional line, its image in
Y_{1}
would have anticanonical degree zero (see Lemma
5.49
).

We note that since
\pi_{1}
has at most finitely many
2
-dimensional fibers, and it is smooth outside these fibers, the composition
\theta:=\beta\circ\pi_{1}\colon W_{1}\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
has at most finitely many reducible fibers, and is equidimensional.
Moreover
\theta
has a second factorization in elementary contractions:

\textstyle{{W_{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\gamma}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\textstyle{{Y_{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{P}^{2}}}

Since
\beta
has one-dimensional fibers, and
\pi_{1}
is finite on fibers of
\gamma
, we see that
\gamma
has fibers of dimension at most
1
.

We show that
\gamma
is of fiber type. By contradiction, suppose that
\gamma
is birational. Since
W_{1}
is Fano with at most isolated, locally factorial, and terminal singularities (see Lemma
5.43
),
\gamma
must be divisorial by
Th.
2.7
, thus it is of type
(3,2)
. Let
E\subset W_{1}
be the exceptional divisor.
We cannot have
\theta(E)=\{pt\}
, because
\theta
is equidimensional. If
\theta(E)
is
a curve in
\mathbb{P}^{2}
, then every fiber of
\theta
over this curve
is reducible, again a contradiction. Therefore
\theta(E)=\mathbb{P}^{2}
.

Set
q:=\beta(p_{2})\in\mathbb{P}^{2}
and
C:=\beta^{-1}(q)\subset Y_{1}
;
then
C
is a smooth rational curve with
-K_{Y_{1}}\cdot C=2
and
p_{2}\in C
.
As in Lemma
5.51
we see that
C
is contained in the open subset where
\pi_{1}
is smooth, and that

S:=\pi_{1}^{-1}(C)\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
, because by
5.60

\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle W}
blows-up
w_{2}
. On the other hand
S=\theta^{-1}(q)
, and

\gamma_{|S}
is a non-trivial birational map, thus we have a contradiction.

Therefore

\gamma
is of fiber type, and has fibers of dimension at most
1
, so
\dim A=3
. We note that
A
is
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial and log Fano, and
\delta\colon A\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
is an elementary contraction, thus it must be equidimensional. Now if
F\subset W_{1}
is a fiber of
\pi_{1}
, then
\gamma
is finite on
F
, and
\gamma(F)
is a fiber of
\delta
. Therefore
\dim F=\dim\gamma(F)=1
, and this concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma 5.62
.

We have
W_{0}\cong\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{P}^{3}}(\mathcal{O}\oplus\mathcal{O}(1))\cong\operatorname{Bl}_{q_{0}}\mathbb{P}^{4}
,
\widehat{W}\cong\operatorname{Bl}_{q_{0},q_{1},\dotsc,q_{r}}\mathbb{P}^{4}
,
and
W
is smooth and is the Fano model of
\operatorname{Bl}_{r+1\,\text{\it pts}}\mathbb{P}^{4}
(see Ex.
7.2
).

We denote by
\sigma_{q_{0}}\colon W_{0}\to\mathbb{P}^{4}
the blow-up map, and set
q_{i}:=\sigma_{q_{0}}(w_{i})\in\mathbb{P}^{4}
for
i=1,\dotsc,r
.

Proof.

Since
\pi_{0}
has no
2
-dimensional fiber by Lemma
5.61
,
we have
T=\emptyset
and

W_{0}=U_{W}
is smooth (see
5.41
and Lemma
5.43
). Moreover

W_{0}=\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{P}^{3}}(\mathcal{E})
where the vector bundle

\mathcal{E}
has odd degree by Lemma
5.59
.

Since
W_{0}
is Fano (see
5.58
),
the possible vector bundles
\mathcal{E}
have been classified in
[
SW90
, Th. (2.1)]
; there is only one case where
\mathcal{E}
is not decomposable (the so-called null-correlation bundle) and it has even degree, so it cannot occur here.
Therefore
\mathcal{E}
is decomposable of odd degree,
and
W_{0}\cong\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{P}^{3}}(\mathcal{O}\oplus\mathcal{O}(b))
with
b\in\{1,3\}
.

If
b=3
, the negative section of
W_{0}\to\mathbb{P}^{3}
is a fixed prime divisor
E
covered by curves of anticanonical degree one, corresponding to lines in
\mathbb{P}^{3}
. Consider the composite birational map
X\dasharrow W_{0}
. By Prop.
2.10

E
must be contained in the open subset where this map is an isomorphism, and the transform
E_{X}\subset X
of
E
is a fixed prime divisor with
E_{X}\cong\mathbb{P}^{3}
and
\mathcal{N}_{E_{X}/E}\cong\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{3}}(-3)
, contradicting Th.-Def.
2.16
.

Thus
b=1
and
W_{0}\cong\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{P}^{3}}(\mathcal{O}\oplus\mathcal{O}(1))\cong\operatorname{Bl}_{q_{0}}\mathbb{P}^{4}
. Let
\sigma_{q_{0}}\colon W_{0}\to\mathbb{P}^{4}
be the blow-up map.
Note that
\operatorname{Exc}(\sigma_{q_{0}})
is covered by curves of anticanonical degree
3
, thus
w_{i}\not\in\operatorname{Exc}(\sigma_{q_{0}})
, otherwise the transform in
\widehat{W}
of such a curve containing
w_{i}
would have anticanonical degree
0
, contradicting Lemma
5.48
. Hence
q_{i}:=\sigma_{q_{0}}(w_{i})\neq q_{0}
for every
i=1,\dotsc,r
,
and
\widehat{W}\cong\operatorname{Bl}_{q_{0},q_{1},\dotsc,q_{r}}\mathbb{P}^{4}
.

Finally
W
is Fano by Lemma
5.6
, and there is a SQM
\zeta\colon W\dasharrow\widehat{W}
(see (
5.47
)), so we get the statement.
∎

5.63
.

The points
q_{0},\dotsc,q_{r}\in\mathbb{P}^{4}
are in general linear position.

In fact if a line in
\mathbb{P}^{4}
contains
3
points among the
q_{i}
’s, then its transform in
\widehat{W}
has anticanonical degree
-4
, which is impossible by Lemma
5.48
. Similarly, if
4
(respectively,
5
) among the
q_{i}
’s are contained in a plane (respectively, a hyperplane), we consider a conic (respectively, a twisted cubic) containing them, and get again a contradiction.

Lemma 5.64
.

We have
r\leq 6
and
\rho_{X}\leq 9
.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.62
,
W
is the Fano model of
\operatorname{Bl}_{r+1\,\text{\it pts}}\mathbb{P}^{4}
; in particular
\rho_{W}\leq 9
, see Ex.
7.2
. Moreover, when
\rho_{W}=9
,
W
does not have non-trivial contractions of fiber type by
[
CCF19
, Prop. 1.7]
.
On the other hand by Lemma
5.22
there is a contraction of fiber type
W\to Z
where
Z
is the anticanonical model of
Y
.
We conclude that
\rho_{W}\leq 8
, hence
\rho_{X}=\rho_{W}+1\leq 9
(see Lemma
5.6
) and
r=\rho_{X}-3\leq 6
.
∎

Recall cases
(a)
and
(b)
from Lemma
5.39
and
5.45
, and that
S_{0}=\alpha_{0}(\operatorname{Exc}(\alpha_{0}))\subset W_{0}
(see
5.41
). For the reader’s convenience, we report here diagram (
5.47
).

Lemma 5.66
.

Set
A:=\sigma_{q_{0}}(S_{0})\subset\mathbb{P}^{4}
. Then

q_{0},\dotsc,q_{r}\in A
, and at these points
A
is smooth in case
(a)
, has rational double points of type
A_{1}
or
A_{2}
in case
(b)
.

Proof.

For
i=1,\dotsc,r
we have
w_{i}\in S_{0}
(see
5.44
) thus
q_{i}=\sigma_{q_{0}}(w_{i})\in A
. Moreover, since
q_{0}\neq q_{i}
, at
q_{i}
the surface
A
is locally isomorphic to
S_{0}
at
w_{i}
, and we deduce the statement from
5.45
.

We are left to prove the behaviour of
A
at the point
q_{0}
.
The map
\pi\colon\widetilde{W}\to Y
(see (
5.65
)) is a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle induced by the projection
\pi_{q_{0}}\colon\mathbb{P}^{4}\dasharrow\mathbb{P}^{3}
from
q_{0}
. The projection of
A
is the surface
B_{0}\subset\mathbb{P}^{3}
which has degree
2
or
4
by Lemma
5.39
; in particular
A
cannot be a plane.
If
A
is a cone, up to exchanging
q_{1}
and
q_{2}
we can assume that
q_{1}
is not the vertex.

Let us consider now the projection
\pi_{q_{1}}\colon\mathbb{P}^{4}\dasharrow\mathbb{P}^{3}
from
q_{1}
, and the corresponding
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle
\pi_{0}^{\prime}\colon W_{0}^{\prime}:=\operatorname{Bl}_{q_{1}}\mathbb{P}^{4}\to\mathbb{P}^{3}
. If
p_{0}^{\prime},p_{2}^{\prime},\dotsc,p_{r}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{P}^{3}
are the images of
q_{0},q_{2},\dotsc,q_{r}\in\mathbb{P}^{4}
via
\pi_{q_{1}}
, we consider
Y^{\prime}:=\operatorname{Bl}_{p_{0}^{\prime},p_{2}^{\prime},\dotsc,p_{r}^{\prime}}\mathbb{P}^{3}
and the composite maps
\widehat{W}=\operatorname{Bl}_{q_{0},\dotsc,q_{r}}\mathbb{P}^{4}\dasharrow Y^{\prime}

and
f^{\prime}_{\scriptscriptstyle X}\colon X\dasharrow Y^{\prime}
(compare with diagram (
5.65
));

\widehat{W}\dasharrow Y^{\prime}
is an elementary rational contraction, and

f_{\scriptscriptstyle X}^{\prime}
is a rational contraction with
\rho_{X}-\rho_{Y^{\prime}}=2
.

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{\scriptscriptstyle X}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{{\widehat{W}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle W}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{{W_{0}^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{0}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{P}^{4}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{q_{1}}}
\textstyle{{Y^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}^{3}}

We show that
f_{\scriptscriptstyle X}^{\prime}
is again special (Def.
3.1
and
3.10
) . If
D\subset X
is a prime divisor different from
E_{1}^{\prime}
, then its transform in
\widehat{W}
is a prime divisor, and its image in
Y^{\prime}
is either
Y^{\prime}
, or a prime divisor. On the other hand
the image of
E_{1}^{\prime}\subset X
in
Y^{\prime}
is the transform, in
Y^{\prime}
, of the projection of
A
from
q_{1}
in
\mathbb{P}^{3}
. Since
A
is not a cone with vertex
q_{1}
, this projection is a surface.

Therefore
we can
replace
f_{\scriptscriptstyle X}
with
f_{\scriptscriptstyle X}^{\prime}
, and get the statement for
q_{0}
too.
∎

Recall from Lemma
5.48
that

S\subset\widehat{W}
is the transform of
S_{0}\subset\operatorname{Bl}_{q_{0}}\mathbb{P}^{4}
and hence of
A\subset\mathbb{P}^{4}
, and
we still denote by
S
its transform in
W
and in
\widetilde{W}
. Moreover
\sigma_{|S}\colon S\to A
is the blow-up of
q_{0},\dotsc,q_{r}\in A
.

5.67
.

We have
\dim\mathcal{N}_{1}(S,\widetilde{W})=\rho_{W}
.

Indeed
A
contains
q_{0},\dotsc,q_{r}
, therefore
S\subset\widehat{W}
meets along a curve every exceptional divisor of the blow-up
\widehat{W}\to\mathbb{P}^{4}
, thus
\mathcal{N}_{1}(S,\widehat{W})=\mathcal{N}_{1}(\widehat{W})
. Moreover
S
is contained in
\operatorname{dom}(\psi_{\scriptscriptstyle W})
(see Lemma
5.48
), therefore
\dim\mathcal{N}_{1}(S,\widetilde{W})=\dim\mathcal{N}_{1}(S,\widehat{W})=\rho_{W}
, see
[
Cas13
, Rem. 3.13(1)]
.

Lemma 5.68
.

Assume that we are in case
(a)
. Then
A
is either a cubic scroll, or a cone over a twisted cubic; moreover
f
and
f_{0}
have some
2
-dimensional fiber.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.39
in case
(a)
the surface
B_{0}\subset\mathbb{P}^{3}
is a quadric, and it is the projection of
A\subset\mathbb{P}^{4}
from the smooth point
q_{0}
(see Lemma
5.66
), thus
A
has degree
3
.
Moreover
A
cannot be contained in a hyperplane, because the points
q_{0},\dotsc,q_{r}\in A
are in general linear position (see
5.63
). By the classification of projective varieties of minimal degree, we conclude that
A
is either a cubic scroll, or a cone over a twisted cubic.

In both cases there is a line through
q_{0}
contained in
A
, thus
\pi_{0}
is not finite on
S_{0}
, and
f_{0}
and
f
have some
2
-dimensional fiber.
∎

5.69
.

Assume that we are in case
(a)
. If
A
is a cubic scroll, then
S
is smooth, because
S\cong\operatorname{Bl}_{q_{0},\dotsc,q_{r}}A
.

5.70
.

Assume that we are in case
(a)
and that
A
is a cone over a twisted cubic. Then by Lemma
5.66

q_{0},\dotsc,q_{r}
are distinct from the vertex
v
of the cone, and
S\cong\operatorname{Bl}_{q_{0},\dotsc,q_{r}}A
has one singular point
v
, which is of type
\frac{1}{3}(1,1)
. Then
\alpha^{-1}(v)\cong\tilde{\alpha}^{-1}(v)\cong\mathbb{P}^{2}
by
[
AW98
, Th. on p. 256]
.
Moreover

B_{0}
is a quadric cone with vertex
v^{\prime}\neq p_{i}
for
i=1,\dotsc,r
, which gives a node
v^{\prime}\in B\subset Y
. The fiber
\pi^{-1}(v^{\prime})
is the transform of the line
\overline{vq_{0}}\subset A
, it is contained in
S\subset\widetilde{W}
, and
f^{-1}(v^{\prime})
has two irreducible components, both of dimension
2
, given by
\tilde{\alpha}^{-1}(v)
and by the closure of
\tilde{\alpha}^{-1}(\pi^{-1}(v^{\prime})\smallsetminus\{v\})
.

Lemma 5.71
.

Assume that we are in case
(b)
. Then
\alpha_{0}
has no
2
-dimensional fiber,
\alpha
and
\tilde{\alpha}
are of type
(3,2)^{\text{\it sm}}
,
S
is smooth,
\operatorname{Sing}(S_{0})=\{w_{1},\dotsc,w_{r}\}
, and
\operatorname{Sing}(A)=\{q_{0},\dotsc,q_{r}\}
.

Proof.

Let us consider both factorizations of
f_{0}
in elementary contractions:

(5.72)

\textstyle{{X_{0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{0}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{0}}
\scriptstyle{f_{0}}
\textstyle{{W_{0}^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{0}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{{W_{0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{0}}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{P}^{3}}}

By Lemma
5.46
in case
(b)
the situation is symmetric, and both
\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle W}
and
\sigma_{W}^{\prime}
blow-up
r
points. Thus we can apply Lemma
5.61
and deduce that both
\pi_{0}
and
\pi_{0}^{\prime}
have no
2
-dimensional fibers. In turn this implies that
\alpha_{0}
and
\alpha_{0}^{\prime}

do not have
2
-dimensional fibers either, because if
F
were such a fiber (for instance for
\alpha_{0}
), then
\alpha_{0}^{\prime}
would be finite on
F
and
\alpha_{0}^{\prime}(F)
would be a
2
-dimensional fiber of
\pi_{0}^{\prime}
.

By Lemma
5.30
every fiber of
f
over
G_{i}
has dimension one, and
\pi^{-1}(G_{i})=\tilde{\alpha}(D_{i})
, therefore every fiber of
\tilde{\alpha}
over
S\cap\tilde{\alpha}(D_{i})\subset\widetilde{W}
has dimension one. Moreover
S
is contained in
\operatorname{dom}(\psi_{\scriptscriptstyle W})
(Lemma
5.48
), and
\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle W}
is an isomorphism between
\widehat{W}\smallsetminus\cup_{i}D_{{\scriptscriptstyle W},i}
and
W_{0}\smallsetminus\{w_{1},\dotsc,w_{r}\}
, hence
\tilde{\alpha}
and
\alpha_{0}
are isomorphic over
S\smallsetminus\cup_{i}\tilde{\alpha}(D_{i})
and
S_{0}\smallsetminus\{w_{1},\dotsc,w_{r}\}
(see diagram (
5.65
)). We conclude that
\tilde{\alpha}
has only one-dimensional fibers, and the same holds for
\alpha
(see Lemma
5.6
).

Then
\alpha
and
\tilde{\alpha}
are of type
(3,2)^{\text{\it sm}}
, and
S
is smooth, by Th.
2.6
.
This also implies that

S_{0}\smallsetminus\{w_{1},\dotsc,w_{r}\}
is smooth, and finally
A\smallsetminus\{q_{0},\dotsc,q_{r}\}
is isomorphic, via
\sigma_{q_{0}}
, to an open subset of
S_{0}\smallsetminus\{w_{1},\dotsc,w_{r}\}
, hence it is smooth too.
∎

Lemma 5.73
.

Assume that we are in case
(b)
. Then

S
is a smooth K3 surface,
B
is a nodal K3 surface, and
\pi_{|S}\colon S\to B
is birational and may contract some smooth fiber of
\pi
to nodes
b\in B
. This happens if and only if
\dim f^{-1}(b)=2
; in this case
f^{-1}(b)\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

Proof.

Recall from
5.44
that
\pi_{0|S_{0}}\colon S_{0}\to B_{0}
is birational, with exceptional locus the fibers of
\pi_{0}
contained in
S_{0}
, and it is an isomorphism around
w_{1},\dotsc,w_{r}
.

By Lemma
5.39
the surface
B_{0}\subset\mathbb{P}^{3}
is a quartic with isolated singularities, so that it is a normal K3 surface.
If
\pi_{0}
is finite on
S_{0}
, then
S_{0}\cong B_{0}
,
\operatorname{Sing}(B_{0})=\{p_{1},\dotsc,p_{r}\}
by Lemma
5.71
, and similarly
S\cong B
are smooth K3 surfaces, so we have the statement.

Suppose that
there is a fiber
F_{0}:=\pi_{0}^{-1}(b)
contained in
S_{0}
; note that
F_{0}\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Then
b\neq p_{i}
and
w_{i}\not\in F_{0}
for every
i
, hence

F_{0}\subset(S_{0})_{\text{\it reg}}
by Lemma
5.71
. Since
K_{B_{0}}=0
, we have
K_{S_{0}}\cdot F_{0}=mF_{0}^{2}
for some
m\in\mathbb{Z}
,
F_{0}^{2}<0
, and by the genus formula
-2=(1+m)F_{0}^{2}
, thus
F_{0}^{2}\in\{-1,-2\}
.

We claim that
F_{0}^{2}=-2
. By contradiction, if
F_{0}^{2}=-1
, then
-K_{S_{0}}\cdot F_{0}=1
, while

-K_{W_{0}}\cdot F_{0}=2
, thus

(\det\mathcal{N}_{S_{0}/W_{0}})\cdot F_{0}=1
. By Lemma
5.71
and Th.
2.6
, in
W_{0}\smallsetminus\{w_{1},\dotsc,w_{r}\}

\alpha_{0}
is just the blow-up of the smooth surface
S_{0}\smallsetminus\{w_{1},\dotsc,w_{r}\}
, hence

R:=\alpha_{0}^{-1}(F_{0})\cong\mathbb{P}_{F_{0}}(\mathcal{N}_{S_{0}/W_{0}}^{\vee})_{|F_{0}}
. By Rem.
2.5
we get
R\cong\mathbb{F}_{e}
with
e\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
odd. Hence
(\alpha^{\prime}_{0})_{|R}
must be birational (see diagram (
5.72
) and the proof of Lemma
5.71
), and
\alpha_{0}^{\prime}(R)
is a
2
-dimensional fiber of
\pi_{0}^{\prime}
, a contradiction.
Therefore
F_{0}^{2}=-2
and
b
is a node for
B_{0}
; moreover
m=0
,
f_{0}^{-1}(b)\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
, and
K_{S_{0}}=0
.

We have a diagram of birational maps:

\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{{\scriptscriptstyle W}|S}}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{|S}}
\textstyle{{S_{0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{0|S}}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{q_{0}|S_{0}}}
\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{k_{|B}}
\textstyle{B_{0}}

where on the first row
S
is smooth,

S_{0}
has rational double points of type
A_{1}
or
A_{2}
at
w_{1},\dotsc,w_{r}
(Lemma
5.71
and
5.45
), and
A
has rational double points of type
A_{1}
or
A_{2}
at
q_{0},\dotsc,q_{r}
. Moreover

B_{0}
has rational double points of type
A_{1}
or
A_{2}
at
p_{1},\dotsc,p_{r}
and nodes at the points
b_{j}
such that
\pi_{0}^{-1}(b_{j})\subset S_{0}
, and
the map
k_{|B}\colon B\to B_{0}
resolves
p_{1},\dotsc,p_{r}
(
5.31
), while it is an isomorphism around the nodes
b_{j}
. Therefore
B
can have at most nodes at the inverse images of
b_{j}
, and
\pi_{|S}\colon S\to B
is a minimal resolution of singularities. Since
B\in|-K_{Y}|
(Lemma
5.39
),
B
is a nodal K3 surface, and
S
is a smooth K3 surface.

Conversely, if
b^{\prime}\in B
is such that
\dim f^{-1}(b^{\prime})=2
, then
b^{\prime}\not\in\operatorname{Exc}(k)
(Lemma
5.30
) and if
b^{\prime\prime}:=k(b^{\prime})
, then
\dim f_{0}^{-1}(b^{\prime\prime})=2
, but both
\pi_{0}
and
\alpha_{0}
have only one-dimensional fibers (Lemma
5.71
). Therefore we must have
\pi_{0}^{-1}(b^{\prime\prime})\subset S_{0}
and
b^{\prime\prime}=b_{j}
for some
j
.
∎

Lemma 5.74
.

Assume that we are in case
(b)
. Then
r=4
and
\rho_{X}=7
, and

A\subset\mathbb{P}^{4}
is a (singular) sextic K3 surface.

Proof.

Since
A
has a double point at
q_{0}
by Lemma
5.66
, and the projection of
A
from
q_{0}
is a quartic surface
B_{0}\subset\mathbb{P}^{3}
(Lemma
5.39
),

A
must have degree
6
. Then
A
is contained in a unique quadric hypersurface
Q
, it is the complete intersection of
Q
with a cubic hypersurface
M
(see for instance
[
Bea96
, Ex. VIII.14]
), and has trivial canonical class.

We note that
Q
must be smooth at
q_{i}
for every
i=0,\dotsc,r
.
Indeed if
Q
is singular at
q_{i}
, then it is a cone, and its projection from
q_{i}
is a quadric in
\mathbb{P}^{3}
. On the other hand
A
cannot be a cone, because
\operatorname{Sing}(A)=\{q_{0},\dotsc,q_{r}\}
(see Lemma
5.71
).
Then, as observed in the proof of Lemma
5.66
, the projection
\pi_{q_{i}}\colon\mathbb{P}^{4}\dasharrow\mathbb{P}^{3}
induces a different special rational contraction
X\dasharrow Y^{i}
with
\rho_{X}-\rho_{Y^{i}}=2
, to which our results apply. In particular the projection of
A
from
q_{i}
must be again a quartic surface in
\mathbb{P}^{3}
, but this is impossible
as
A\subset Q
. Therefore
Q
must be smooth at
q_{i}
.

Since
A
has double points at
q_{0},\dotsc,q_{r}
(Lemma
5.66
), in turn the cubic
M
must have a double point at each
q_{i}
.

Suppose by contradiction that
r>4
, let
q\in M
be a general point,
and let
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{P}^{4}
be the rational normal quartic through
q_{0},q_{1},\dotsc,q_{5},q
. Then
\Gamma\cdot M=12
, and
\Gamma
and
M
intersect with multiplicity
\geq 1
in
q
and
\geq 2
in
q_{0},\dotsc,q_{5}
, thus
\Gamma\subset M
.
Moreover
\Gamma
intersects the quadric
Q
in
q_{0},\dotsc,q_{5}
plus two additional points
a
and
b
, that belong to
A
.

Let
\widehat{\Gamma}\subset\widehat{W}
,
\Gamma_{W}\subset W
, and
\Gamma_{X}\subset X
be the transforms of
\Gamma
. Then

-K_{\widehat{W}}\cdot\widehat{\Gamma}=20-18=2
, and

\widehat{\Gamma}
intersects
S
in two points, corresponding to
a
and
b
.
Recall that
S\subset\operatorname{dom}(\zeta^{-1})
(see Lemma
5.48
), thus
\Gamma_{W}
still intersects
S
in two points. Moreover
-K_{W}\cdot\Gamma_{W}\leq-K_{\widehat{W}}\cdot\widehat{\Gamma}=2
(see
[
Cas13
, Rem. 3.6]
),
thus
-K_{X}\cdot\Gamma_{X}\leq 0
, a contradiction.
∎

This concludes the proof of Th.
5.1
.
∎

6.
The case
\delta_{X}=2
and the elementary case

In this section we treat Fano
4
-folds with Lefschetz defect
2
, and Fano
4
-folds with an elementary rational contraction onto a
3
-fold.
For the case where
\delta_{X}=2
, we show the following more refined version of Th.
1.8
.

Theorem 6.1
.

Let
X
be a smooth Fano
4
-fold with
\delta_{X}=2
. Then
3\leq\rho_{X}\leq 6
, and one of the following holds:

(i)

there is a special rational contraction
X\dasharrow Y
onto a smooth
3
-fold with
\rho_{X}-\rho_{Y}=2
;

(ii)

there is a quasi-elementary contraction
X\to S
where either
S\cong\mathbb{P}^{2}
and
\rho_{X}=4
, or
S\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
or
\mathbb{F}_{1}
and
\rho_{X}=5
.

A contraction
X\to S
is

quasi-elementary
if for every fiber
F\subset X
we have
\dim\mathcal{N}_{1}(F,X)=\rho_{X}-\rho_{S}
; we refer the reader to
[
Cas08
, §3]
for more details.

The bound
\rho_{X}\leq 6
improves the previous bound
\rho_{X}\leq 12
, see
[
Cas17
, Th. 2.12 and references therein]
, and is sharp, by the example
(\operatorname{Bl}_{2\text{\it pts}}\mathbb{P}^{2})^{2}
(see Rem.
2.1
). On the other hand we are not aware of other examples of Fano
4
-folds with
\delta_{X}=2
and
\rho_{X}=6
, while for
\rho_{X}=4,5
we provide several (known and new) examples in §
7.4
and §
7.6
. Fano
4
-folds with
\delta_{X}=2
and
\rho_{X}=3
are classified and studied in
[
Sec23
]
, there are 28 families.

Proof of Th.
6.1
.

By
[
Cas14
, Th. 5.2 and its proof]

one of the following holds:

(1)

there exist
a SQM
X\dasharrow\widetilde{X}
, a special,
K
-negative contraction
f\colon\widetilde{X}\to Y
, and
a prime divisor
D\subset\widetilde{X}
, such that
Y
is smooth,

\dim Y=3
,
\rho_{X}-\rho_{Y}=2
,
\operatorname{codim}\mathcal{N}_{1}(D,\widetilde{X})=2
,
and
f({D})=Y
;

(2)

there is a quasi-elementary contraction
\psi\colon X\to S
with
\dim S=2
and
\rho_{X}-\rho_{S}=3
.

Assume first that we are in
(1)
, and suppose by contradiction that
\rho_{X}\geq 7
. Then
the study made in Section
5
applies to
X\dasharrow Y
; let us
consider the factorization of
f
as in Lemma
5.6
and
5.60
:

\textstyle{{\widetilde{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{\alpha}}
\textstyle{{\widetilde{W}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{{Y}}

where
\tilde{\alpha}
is an elementary contraction of type
(3,2)
with

E_{1}=\operatorname{Exc}(\tilde{\alpha})\subset\widetilde{X}
and

S=\tilde{\alpha}(E_{1})\subset\widetilde{W}
.

We show that
\mathcal{N}_{1}(S,\widetilde{W})\subsetneq\mathcal{N}_{1}(\widetilde{W})
, which contradicts

5.67
.

Since
f({D})=Y
, we have
f_{*}(\mathcal{N}_{1}({D},\widetilde{X}))=\mathcal{N}_{1}(Y)
, but
\dim\mathcal{N}_{1}({D},\widetilde{X})=\dim\mathcal{N}_{1}(Y)=\rho_{X}-2
, therefore
\mathcal{N}_{1}({D},\widetilde{X})\cap\ker f_{*}=\{0\}
, in particular
\operatorname{NE}(\tilde{\alpha})\not\subset\mathcal{N}_{1}({D},\widetilde{X})
. This implies that
\tilde{\alpha}
must be finite on
{D}
.

If
{D}\cap E_{1}\neq\emptyset
, then
{D}\cdot\operatorname{NE}(\tilde{\alpha})>0
, so that
{D}
meets every non-trivial fiber of
\tilde{\alpha}
, and the prime divisor

\tilde{\alpha}({D})
contains
S
. We have

\mathcal{N}_{1}(\tilde{\alpha}({D}),\widetilde{W})=\tilde{\alpha}_{*}(\mathcal{N}_{1}({D},\widetilde{X}))
, thus
\dim\mathcal{N}_{1}(\tilde{\alpha}({D}),\widetilde{W})\leq\dim\mathcal{N}_{1}({D},\widetilde{X})=\rho_{X}-2=\rho_{W}-1
, and
\mathcal{N}_{1}(S,\widetilde{W})\subseteq\mathcal{N}_{1}(\tilde{\alpha}({D}),\widetilde{W})\subsetneq\mathcal{N}_{1}(\widetilde{W})
. If instead
{D}\cap E_{1}=\emptyset
, then
\tilde{\alpha}({D})\cap S=\emptyset
, thus
\mathcal{N}_{1}(S,\widetilde{W})\subseteq\tilde{\alpha}({D})^{\perp}\subsetneq\mathcal{N}_{1}(\widetilde{W})
(see Rem.
2.2
).

We conclude that in case
(1)
we have
\rho_{X}\leq 6
, therefore we get
(i)
.

Assume now that we are in
(2)
.
By
[
Cas08
, Th. 1.1]

S
is a smooth del Pezzo surface, so if
\rho_{S}\leq 2
we have
(ii)
. If instead

\rho_{S}\geq 3
, then again by
[
Cas08
, Th. 1.1]
we have
X\cong S\times F
where
F=\operatorname{Bl}_{2\text{\it pts}}\mathbb{P}^{2}
, and
\delta_{X}=\max\{\rho_{S}-1,\rho_{F}-1\}
(see Rem.
2.1
). Hence

\rho_{S}-1\leq\delta_{X}=2
, which gives
\rho_{S}=3
and
S\cong F
, and we have again
(i)
with the contraction
S\times S\to\mathbb{P}^{1}\times S
.
∎

Corollary 6.2
.

Let
X
be a smooth Fano
4
-fold that is not isomorphic to a product of surfaces. If
\delta_{X}\geq 2
,
then
\rho_{X}\leq 6
.

Proof.

Since
X
is not a product of surfaces, by
Th.
1.6
and
1.7
we have
\delta_{X}\leq 3
, and if
\delta_{X}=3
, then
\rho_{X}\leq 6
. Moreover if
\delta_{X}=2
, then again
\rho_{X}\leq 6
by Th.
1.8
.
∎

In the case of an elementary rational contraction onto a
3
-fold, we show the following.

Theorem 6.3
.

Let
X
be a smooth Fano
4
-fold that is not isomorphic to a product of surfaces, and having an elementary rational contraction

X\dasharrow Y
with
\dim Y=3
.
Then
\rho_{X}\leq 9
.

The bound is sharp, as shown by the Fano model of
\operatorname{Bl}_{8\text{\it pts}}\mathbb{P}^{4}
, see Ex.
7.2
.

Proof of Th.
6.3
.

Since
X
is not a product of surfaces, by
Th.
1.6
and
1.7
we have
\delta_{X}\leq 3
, and if
\delta_{X}=3
, then
\rho_{X}\leq 6
. Therefore we can assume that
X
has Lefschetz defect
\delta_{X}\leq 2
.

We follow
[
Cas13
, §4, in particular the proof of Th. 4.4]
; the strategy is the same as the beginning of the proof of Th.
5.1
.
We know that
Y
has at most isolated, locally factorial, canonical singularities.
Moreover
Y
is log Fano,
-K_{Y}
is big, and if
g\colon Y\to Y_{0}
is an elementary contraction of fiber type, then
-K_{Y}\cdot\operatorname{NE}(g)>0
. By
[
Cas13
, Lemma 4.5]
, if
g\colon Y\to Y_{0}
is a small elementary contraction, then
K_{Y}\cdot\operatorname{NE}(g)=0
.

Assume now that
\rho_{X}\geq 6
.
Then by
[
Cas13
, Lemma 4.6]
, if
g\colon Y\to Y_{0}
is a divisorial elementary contraction, then
-K_{Y}\cdot\operatorname{NE}(g)>0
and
g
is the blow-up of a smooth point (note that in
loc. cit.
the map
X\dasharrow Y
is assumed to be non-regular, but this is used only to deduce that
\delta_{X}\leq 2
, which we already know).
In particular
Y
is weak Fano, and as in
5.14
we show that if
Y
is not Fano, then its anticanonical map is small.

As in
[
Cas13
, p. 622]
we consider all divisorial extremal rays of
\operatorname{NE}(Y)
and get a map

k\colon Y\longrightarrow Y_{0}

which is the blow-up of
r
distinct smooth points. Moreover
Y_{0}
is weak Fano, has the same singularities as
Y
,
\rho_{Y}=\rho_{Y_{0}}+r
, and
(-K_{Y})^{3}=(-K_{Y_{0}})^{3}-8r
.

As in the proof of Lemma
5.16

we show that, up to increasing the number
r
of blown-up points, and up to replacing
Y
and
Y_{0}
with SQM’s, we can reduce to the case where
\rho_{Y_{0}}\leq 2
and, if
\rho_{Y_{0}}=2
, then
Y_{0}
has two distinct elementary rational contractions of fiber type. Moreover as in Lemma
5.15
we show that, if
Y_{0}
is not Fano, then its anticanonical map is small, with exceptional locus contained in
(Y_{0})_{\text{\it reg}}
.

If
\rho_{Y_{0}}=1
, then
Y_{0}
is Fano, and Lemma
4.1
yields

-K_{Y_{0}}^{3}\leq 64
. Thus

0<-K_{Y}^{3}=-K_{Y_{0}}^{3}-8r\leq 64-8r

which gives
r\leq 7
,
\rho_{Y}=r+\rho_{Y_{0}}\leq 8
, and
\rho_{X}=\rho_{Y}+1\leq 9
.
If instead
\rho_{Y_{0}}=2
, then by Lemma
4.2
and Prop.
4.3
we have either
Y_{0}\cong\mathbb{P}^{2}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
, or
Y_{0}\cong\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(\mathcal{O}\oplus\mathcal{O}(1)^{\oplus 2})
, or

-K_{Y_{0}}^{3}\leq 48
. The blow-up of
\mathbb{P}^{2}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
or
\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(\mathcal{O}\oplus\mathcal{O}(1)^{\oplus 2})
at a point (not lying on the curve of anticanonical degree zero) has a divisorial elementary contraction of type
(2,1)
, which is excluded as in Lemma
5.15
. Therefore
-K_{Y_{0}}^{3}\leq 48
,
and as above we get
r\leq 5
,
\rho_{Y}\leq 7
, and
\rho_{X}\leq 8
.
∎

As in the proof of Lemma
5.26
, we also get the following.

Corollary 6.4
.

Let
X
be a smooth Fano
4
-fold and

X\dasharrow Y
an elementary rational contraction with
\dim Y=3
. Assume that
\rho_{X}\geq 6
,

\delta_{X}\leq 2
, and that
Y
is smooth.

Then
Y
is weak Fano and, up to flops,
Y\cong\operatorname{Bl}_{r\,\text{\it pts}}Y_{0}
where
Y_{0}
is one of the following.

\begin{array}[]{||c|c|c|c|c||}\hline\cr\hline\cr Y_{0}&-K_{Y_{0}}^{3}&\rho_{Y_{0}}&&\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr\mathbb{P}^{3}&64&1&\text{Fano}&\rho_{X}\leq 9,r=\rho_{X}-2\\
\hline\cr\mathbb{P}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(T_{\mathbb{P}^{2}})&48&2&\text{Fano}&\rho_{X}\leq 8,r=\rho_{X}-3\\
\hline\cr\text{\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{jahnkepeternell}{}{}, Th.\penalty\ 3.6(1)]}}&40&2&\text{weak Fano}&\rho_{X}\leq 7,r=\rho_{X}-3\\
\hline\cr\text{linear section of }G(2,5)&40&1&\text{Fano}&\rho_{X}=6,r=4\\
\hline\cr\text{\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{jahnkepeternell}{}{}, Th.\penalty\ 3.5(3)]}}&32&2&\text{weak Fano}&\rho_{X}=6,r=3\\
\hline\cr\text{divisor of degree $(1,2)$ in }\mathbb{P}^{2}\times\mathbb{P}^{2}&30&2&\text{Fano}&\rho_{X}=6,r=3\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr\end{array}

Proof of Th.
1.3
.

Since
X
is not a product of surfaces, by
Cor.
6.2

if
\delta_{X}\geq 2
then
\rho_{X}\leq 6
. Therefore we can assume that
X
has Lefschetz defect
\delta_{X}\leq 1
.

By Prop.
3.11
we can assume that the rational
contraction
X\dasharrow Y
is special.
Then
\rho_{X}-\rho_{Y}\in\{1,2\}
by Lemma
3.12
, because

\delta_{X}\leq 1
. Thus the statement follows from Th.
6.3
and
5.1
.
∎

Proof of Th.
1.10
.

Since
\rho_{X}\geq 7
and
X
is not a product of surfaces, Cor.
6.2
implies that
\delta_{X}\leq 1
. As in the proof above, we see that there is a special rational
contraction
X\dasharrow Y
with
\dim Y=3
and
\rho_{X}-\rho_{Y}\in\{1,2\}
. Then
Y
is weak Fano with at most isolated, locally factorial, and canonical singularities, by the proofs of Th.
6.3
and
5.1
(in particular
5.4
and
5.12
).
∎

7.
New families and examples

In this section we construct several new families of Fano
4
-folds, with Picard number between
2
and
7
, all having a rational contraction onto a
3
-fold.
Our strategy is inspired by the classification result in Th.
1.11
and more generally by the study in Section
5
of Fano
4
-folds with a special rational contraction
X\dasharrow Y
with
\rho_{X}-\rho_{Y}=2
.

In §
7.2
and §
7.3
respectively, we show that cases
(i)
and
(ii)
of Th.
1.11
do happen for
\rho_{X}\in\{3,\dotsc,7\}
.
This proves Prop.
1.9
, and leaves open case
(iii)
, as follows.

Question 7.1
.

Let
r\in\{0,\dotsc,6\}
and let
W
be the Fano model of
\operatorname{Bl}_{q_{0},\dotsc,q_{r}}\mathbb{P}^{4}
, for
q_{i}
general points (see Ex.
7.2
).
Let
A\subset\mathbb{P}^{4}
be a general cone over a twisted cubic containing
q_{0},\dotsc,q_{r}
.
Let
S\subset W
be the transform of
A
, and
X\to W
the blow-up of
S
.
Is
X
is a smooth Fano
4
-fold?

We also leave open the existence of case
(i)
of Th.
1.11
for
\rho_{X}=8,9
, see Question
7.6
.

We also give other constructions.
In §
7.4
we adapt case
(a)
from Section
5
to get examples of Fano
4
-folds with Lefschetz defect
2
and
\rho\in\{3,4,5\}
. Then in §
7.5
we show that the examples from §
7.2
have a different blow-down to a smooth Fano
4
-fold
Z
with
\rho_{Z}\in\{2,\dotsc,6\}
, and we give an explicit description of
Z
thanks to a result in
[
Lan98
]
. Finally in §
7.6
we recall some other known examples.

For all the new families we compute the main numerical invariants, using Lemma
2.4
and
[
CCF19
, Lemma 6.25]
, see Tables
7.2
,
7.3
,
7.4
,
7.5
.

In most cases these new families are obtained as blow-ups of the Fano model of
\operatorname{Bl}_{\text{\it pts}}\mathbb{P}^{4}
along a surface, so let us start by recalling this example.

Example 7.2

(the Fano model of
\operatorname{Bl}_{\text{\it pts}}\mathbb{P}^{4}
)
.

Let
\sigma\colon\widehat{W}\to\mathbb{P}^{4}
be the blow-up at
r+1
general points
q_{0},\dotsc,q_{r}
, with
r\in\{0,\dotsc,7\}
. For
r\geq 1
the
4
-fold
\widehat{W}
is not Fano, but there is a SQM
\widehat{W}\dasharrow W
such that
W
is smooth and Fano, with
\rho_{W}=r+2\in\{2,\dotsc,9\}
; we refer to
W
as the Fano model of
\operatorname{Bl}_{r+1\,\text{\it pts}}\mathbb{P}^{4}
.

The blow-up
\widehat{W}
contains exceptional lines given by the transforms of the lines
\overline{q_{i}q_{j}}
, and (for
r=6,7
) of the rational normal quartics through
7
points among
q_{0},\dotsc,q_{r}
; these curves are the indeterminacy locus of the map
\widehat{W}\dasharrow W
(see Lemma
2.13
(a)
).

We note that
W
is toric if and only if
r\leq 4
; in the classification of toric Fano
4
-folds in
[
Bat99
]
, these are
B_{3}
,
D_{9}
,
M_{1}
, 3.5.8(iii), and 3.5.8(ii). We refer the reader to
[
AC17
]
for more details on the case
r=6
, and to
[
CCF19
]
for the case
r=7
.
For
r\geq 8
it has been shown by Mukai
[
Muk01
,
Muk04
]
that
\widehat{W}
is not a Mori dream space, and this is a necessary condition to have a SQM that is smooth and Fano.

Consider the projection
\mathbb{P}^{4}\dasharrow\mathbb{P}^{3}
from
q_{0}
(an analogous construction can be made for the other points
q_{j}
), let
p_{i}\in\mathbb{P}^{3}
be the image of
q_{i}
for
i=1,\dotsc,r
, and
let
k\colon Y\to\mathbb{P}^{3}
be the blow-up of
p_{1},\dotsc,p_{r}
.
Then the composition
W\dasharrow Y
is an elementary rational contraction.
More precisely, there is an intermediate SQM
\widehat{W}\dasharrow\widetilde{W}
, that flips the transforms of the lines
\overline{q_{0}q_{i}}
for
i=1,\dotsc,r
, such that
\pi\colon\widetilde{W}\to Y
is a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle (see for instance
[
CCF19
, Rem. 6.12]
).

For
r=7
we have
\rho_{W}=9
, thus this example shows that the bounds on
\rho
in Th.
1.3
and
6.3
are sharp.

We report in Table
7.1
the main numerical invariants of
W
.

\begin{array}[]{||c|c|c|c|c|c|c|c||}\hline\cr\hline\cr r&\rho_{W}&K_{W}^{4}&K_{W}^{2}\cdot c_{2}(W)&b_{4}(W)=h^{2,2}(W)&b_{3}(W)&h^{0}(W,-K_{W})&\chi(T_{W})\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr 0&2&544&232&2&0&111&20\\
\hline\cr 1&3&464&212&4&0&96&16\\
\hline\cr 2&4&385&190&7&0&81&12\\
\hline\cr 3&5&307&166&11&0&66&8\\
\hline\cr 4&6&230&140&16&0&51&4\\
\hline\cr 5&7&154&112&22&0&36&0\\
\hline\cr 6&8&80&80&30&0&21&-4\\
\hline\cr 7&9&13&34&45&0&6&-8\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr\end{array}

Table 7.1.
Numerical invariants of the Fano model
W
of
\operatorname{Bl}_{r+1\,\text{\it pts}}\mathbb{P}^{4}

7.1.
Set-up for blow-ups of
W

In §
7.2
,
7.3
, and
7.4
we are going to construct families of Fano
4
-folds
X
as blow-ups of
W
along a smooth surface; we set up here a common notation.

Let
W
be the Fano model of
\widehat{W}=\operatorname{Bl}_{q_{0},\dotsc,q_{r}}\mathbb{P}^{4}
; we keep the same notation as in Ex.
7.2
. Let
A\subset\mathbb{P}^{4}
be an irreducible surface and
S\subset\widehat{W}
its transform. In all our examples
S
will be smooth and contained in the open subset where the map
\widehat{W}\dasharrow W
is an isomorphism; we still denote by
S\subset W
its transform.

Let
\alpha\colon X\to W
be the blow-up of
S
, and
E\subset X
the exceptional divisor.

We denote by
H
the transform of
\sigma^{*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{4}}(1)
in
W
, and by
H_{X}
its pullback in
X
.

As in Ex.
7.2
consider the projection
\mathbb{P}^{4}\dasharrow\mathbb{P}^{3}
from
q_{0}
and the associated

\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle
\pi\colon\widetilde{W}\to Y=\operatorname{Bl}_{p_{1},\dotsc,p_{r}}\mathbb{P}^{3}
.
Let
k\colon Y\to\mathbb{P}^{3}
be the blow-up map,
G_{i}\subset Y
the exceptional divisor over
p_{i}
, and
H_{Y}:=k^{*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{3}}(1)
.
We assume that
A
is not a cone with vertex
q_{0}
;
let
B_{0}\subset\mathbb{P}^{3}
be the projection of
A
(so that
\dim B_{0}=2
), and
B\subset Y
its transform.
We still denote by
S\subset\widetilde{W}
the transform of
A
; then
B=\pi(S)
.

Let
\tilde{\alpha}\colon\widetilde{X}\to\widetilde{W}
be the blow-up of
S
, and
f:=\pi\circ\tilde{\alpha}\colon\widetilde{X}\to Y
. The induced map
X\dasharrow\widetilde{X}
is a SQM.

(7.3)

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{{\widetilde{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{\alpha}}
\textstyle{W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\widetilde{W}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{{\widehat{W}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{P}^{4}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{k}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{P}^{3}}}

Remark 7.4
.

The composition
X\dasharrow Y
is a special rational contraction with
\rho_{X}-\rho_{Y}=2
.
In particular we deduce from Th.
5.1
that
X
is not Fano when
\rho_{X}>9
and
r>6
.

Let
\widehat{D}_{i}\subset\widehat{W}
be the exceptional divisor of the blow-up
\sigma\colon\widehat{W}\to\mathbb{P}^{4}
over
q_{i}
, and
D_{i}\subset{W}
,
\widetilde{D}_{i}\subset\widetilde{W}
its transforms.

Let
T:=\pi^{-1}(B)\subset\widetilde{W}
and
\widetilde{T}\subset\widetilde{X}
its transform;
since
S
is a smooth surface contained in
T
, we have
\widetilde{T}\cong T
. Note that
T
is the transform of the cone in
\mathbb{P}^{4}
over
A
with vertex
q_{0}
.
In all our examples,

T
will be contained in the open subset where
the birational map
\widetilde{W}\dasharrow W
is an isomorphism. Similarly,

\widetilde{T}
is contained in the open subset where the map
\widetilde{X}\dasharrow X
is an isomorphism; we still denote by
T\subset W
and
\widetilde{T}\subset X
the transforms.

Suppose now that
r\leq 4
; then
W
is toric and
\widetilde{W}=\mathbb{P}_{Y}(\mathcal{E})
with

\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{O}_{Y}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{Y}\bigl(H_{Y}-\sum_{i=1}^{r}G_{i}\bigr).

Let moreover
J\subset\widetilde{W}
be the transform of a hyperplane
J_{0}\subset\mathbb{P}^{4}
through
q_{1},\dotsc,q_{r}
; then
J
is the section of
\pi
corresponding to the projection
\mathcal{E}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{Y}(H_{Y}-\sum_{i=1}^{r}G_{i})
, and

\eta=\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{W}}(J)=\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{W}}(\widetilde{H}-\sum_{i=1}^{r}\widetilde{D}_{i})

is the tautological class, where
\widetilde{H}
is the transform of
\sigma^{*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{4}}(1)
; moreover
\eta_{|J}\cong\mathcal{O}_{Y}(H_{Y}-\sum_{i=1}^{r}G_{i})
.

We also have
T=\mathbb{P}_{B}(\mathcal{E}_{|B})
, with tautological class

\eta_{|T}=\mathcal{O}_{T}(J_{|T})
, and we set
\pi_{T}:=\pi_{|T}\colon T\to B
.

7.2.
New families from case
(a)

We keep the notation as in §
7.1
, and assume that
r\leq 6
(see Rem.
7.4
).
Let
A\subset\mathbb{P}^{4}
be a general cubic rational normal scroll containing

q_{0},\dotsc,q_{r}
(such a scroll exists, see for instance
[
Cos06
, Ex. A]
);
recall that
A\cong\mathbb{F}_{1}
.
Then
S\subset\widehat{W}
is a del Pezzo surface with
\rho_{S}=r+3
, and it is disjoint from the transforms of the lines
\overline{q_{i}q_{j}}
and, for
r=6
, of the rational normal quartic through
q_{0},\dotsc,q_{6}
.
We will show the following.

Proposition 7.5
.

For
r\in\{0,\dotsc,4\}
,
X
is Fano with
\rho_{X}=r+3\in\{3,\dotsc,7\}
.

Question 7.6
.

Is
X
Fano for
r=5,6
?

The surface
B_{0}\subset\mathbb{P}^{3}
is a smooth quadric surface
containing
p_{1},\dotsc,p_{r}
,
B
is a smooth del Pezzo surface with
\rho_{B}=r+2
, and
-K_{Y}=2B
. Moreover

\pi_{|S}\colon S\to B
is the blow-up of a point
y\in B
, with exceptional curve the transform
\Gamma
of the line in
A
containing
q_{0}
. Every fiber of
f
over
Y\smallsetminus\{y\}
is one-dimensional, while
f^{-1}(y)=\tilde{\alpha}^{-1}(\Gamma)\cong\mathbb{F}_{1}
.

Note that
T\subset\widetilde{W}
is the transform of the quadric cone
Q\subset\mathbb{P}^{4}
, containing
A
, with vertex
q_{0}
. Then
T
is disjoint from the transforms of the lines
\overline{q_{i}q_{j}}
for
1\leq i<j\leq r
, thus it is contained in the open subset where the map
\widetilde{W}\dasharrow W
is an isomorphism.

Lemma 7.7
.

For
r\in\{0,\dotsc,4\}
,
-K_{X|\widetilde{T}}
is ample.

Proof.

We treat the case
r>0
, the case
r=0
being similar and simpler.
We have
-K_{X|\widetilde{T}}\cong-K_{\widetilde{X}|\widetilde{T}}
, thus it is enough to show that
-K_{\widetilde{X}|\widetilde{T}}
is ample.

Let us fix some notation in
B
. We have
B=\operatorname{Bl}_{p_{1},\dotsc,p_{r}}B_{0}\cong\operatorname{Bl}_{r+1\,\text{\it pts}}\mathbb{P}^{2}
. We denote by
e_{2},\dotsc,e_{r}\subset B
the exceptional curves over
p_{2},\dotsc,p_{r}\in B_{0}
, so that
e_{i}
are lines in
G_{i}\cong\mathbb{P}^{2}
. Then we denote by
e_{0},e_{1}\subset B
the transforms of the two lines through
p_{1}
in the quadric
B_{0}
, and by
C_{G_{1}}\subset B
the exceptional curve over
p_{1}
, again a line in
G_{1}
. There is a birational map
B\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
contracting
e_{0},e_{1},\dotsc,e_{r}
, let
h\in\operatorname{Pic}(B)
be the pullback of
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(1)
. Then
C_{G_{1}}\sim h-e_{0}-e_{1}
.

We have
\mathcal{O}_{B_{0}}(1)=\mathcal{O}_{B_{0}}(k(e_{0})+k(e_{1}))
,

H_{Y|B}=(k_{|B})^{*}(k(e_{0})+k(e_{1}))=e_{0}+e_{1}+2C_{G_{1}}\sim 2h-e_{0}-e_{1}
, and

\Bigl(H_{Y}-\sum_{i=1}^{r}G_{i}\Bigr)_{|B}\sim(2h-e_{0}-e_{1})-(h-e_{0}-e_{1})-e_{2}-\cdots-e_{r}=h-e_{2}-\cdots-e_{r}.

Thus
\widetilde{T}\cong T=\mathbb{P}_{B}(\mathcal{E}_{|B})
with
\mathcal{E}_{|B}=\mathcal{O}_{B}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{B}(h-e_{2}-\cdots-e_{r})
.

In
T
we have
S\sim\eta_{|T}+\pi_{T}^{*}(M)
for some
M\in\operatorname{Pic}(B)
.
Moreover
-K_{T}=\pi_{T}^{*}(-K_{B}-h+e_{2}+\cdots+e_{r})+2\eta_{|T}
, and
\tilde{\alpha}^{*}(T)=\widetilde{T}+\widetilde{E}
where
\widetilde{E}=\operatorname{Exc}(\tilde{\alpha})\subset\widetilde{X}
. Finally
T=\pi^{*}(B)
and
-K_{Y}=2B
, thus
\mathcal{O}_{Y}(B)_{|B}=-K_{B}
and
\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{W}}(T)_{|T}=\mathcal{O}_{T}(\pi_{T}^{*}(-K_{B}))
.
Then

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{X}(\widetilde{T})_{|\widetilde{T}}=\

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{X}\bigl(\alpha^{*}(T)-\widetilde{E}\bigr)_{|\widetilde{T}}\cong\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{W}}(T)_{|T}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{T}(-S),\text{ and hence:}

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{X}}(-K_{\widetilde{X}})_{|\widetilde{T}}=\

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{T}}(-K_{\widetilde{T}})\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{X}}(\widetilde{T})_{|\widetilde{T}}\cong\mathcal{O}_{T}(-K_{T}-S)\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{W}}(T)_{|T}

\displaystyle\cong\

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{T}\bigl(\pi_{T}^{*}(-2K_{B}-h+e_{2}+\cdots+e_{r})+2\eta_{|T}-S\bigr)

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{T}\bigl(\pi_{T}^{*}(-K_{B}+2h-e_{0}-e_{1}-M)+\eta_{|T}\bigr).

Therefore
-K_{\widetilde{X}|\widetilde{T}}
is isomorphic to the tautological class for

T=\mathbb{P}_{B}\bigl(\mathcal{O}_{B}(-K_{B}+2h-e_{0}-e_{1}-M)\oplus\mathcal{O}_{B}(-2K_{B}-M)\bigr),

and we have to show that both linear summands are ample on
B
.

We compute
M
by restricting to
T\cap J\cong B
the relation
S\sim\eta_{|T}+\pi_{T}^{*}(M)
of divisors in
T
. We have

\eta_{|T\cap J}\cong(\eta_{|J})_{|T\cap J}\cong\Bigl(H_{Y}-\sum_{i=1}^{r}G_{i}\Bigr)_{|B}=h-e_{2}-\cdots-e_{r}.

Now we need to compute the class of
S\cap J
in
T\cap J
.
Since
Q\subset\mathbb{P}^{4}
is a quadric cone with vertex
q_{0}
,
Q\cap J_{0}
is a quadric surface containing
q_{1},\dotsc,q_{r}
, isomorphic to
B_{0}
via the projection from
q_{0}
.
Moreover
C:=A\cap J_{0}
is a twisted cubic in
J_{0}\cong\mathbb{P}^{3}
, and as a curve in
Q\cap J_{0}\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
it has degree
(2,1)
(see for instance
[
Har92
, Ex. 2.16]
). The map
\sigma_{|T\cap J}\colon T\cap J\to Q\cap J_{0}
blows-up
q_{1},\dotsc,q_{r}
. The transform of
C
in
T\cap J\cong B
is
S\cap J
, and has class:

\displaystyle\sigma_{|T\cap J}^{*}\mathcal{O}(2,1)-C_{G_{1}}-e_{2}-\cdots-e_{r}

\displaystyle\sim 2(e_{0}+C_{G_{1}})+(e_{1}+C_{G_{1}})-C_{G_{1}}-e_{2}-\cdots-e_{r}=

\displaystyle=2C_{G_{1}}+2e_{0}+e_{1}-e_{2}-\cdots-e_{r}\sim 2h-e_{1}-\cdots-e_{r}.

We conclude that
M\sim 2h-e_{1}-\cdots-e_{r}-(h-e_{2}-\cdots-e_{r})=h-e_{1}
.
Finally

-K_{B}+2h-e_{0}-e_{1}-M\sim-K_{B}+h-e_{0}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt-2K_{B}-M\sim-K_{B}+2h-e_{0}-e_{2}-\cdots-e_{r}.

Now
h-e_{0}
and
2h-e_{0}-e_{2}-\cdots-e_{r}
are nef (since
r\leq 4
) and
-K_{B}
is ample, so both summands are ample.
∎

Lemma 7.8
.

For
r\in\{0,\dotsc,4\}
,
-K_{X|E}
is ample.

Proof.

We show that
\mathcal{N}_{S/W}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{S}(-K_{S})
is ample; this implies the statement by Lemma
2.4
.
Since
S
is contained in the open subset where the map
W\dasharrow\widetilde{W}
is an isomorphism, it is equivalent to show that
\mathcal{N}_{S/\widetilde{W}}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{S}(-K_{S})
is ample.

Let us consider
S\subset T\subset\widetilde{W}
and the associated normal bundle sequence:

0\longrightarrow\mathcal{N}_{S/T}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{S}(-K_{S})\longrightarrow\mathcal{N}_{S/\widetilde{W}}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{S}(-K_{S})\longrightarrow\mathcal{N}_{T/\widetilde{W}|S}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{S}(-K_{S})\longrightarrow 0.

Recall from the proof of Lemma
7.7
, whose notation we keep, that

\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{W}}(T)_{|T}=\mathcal{O}_{T}(\pi_{T}^{*}(-K_{B}))
. Then
we have
\mathcal{N}_{T/\widetilde{W}|S}=\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{W}}(T)_{|S}=\mathcal{O}_{S}(\pi_{|S}^{*}(-K_{B}))
nef in
S
, thus
\mathcal{N}_{T/\widetilde{W}|S}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{S}(-K_{S})

is ample.

Still from the proof of Lemma
7.7
we have that
S\sim\eta_{|T}+\pi_{T}^{*}(M)
is a tautological divisor for

T=\mathbb{P}_{B}(\mathcal{E}_{|B}\otimes M)=\mathbb{P}_{B}\bigl(\mathcal{O}_{B}(h-e_{1})\oplus\mathcal{O}_{B}(2h-e_{1}-\cdots-e_{r})\bigr).

Both
h-e_{1}
and
2h-e_{1}-\cdots-e_{r}
are nef in
B
(since
r\leq 4
), thus

S
is nef in
T
, and
\mathcal{N}_{S/T}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{S}(-K_{S})=\mathcal{O}_{T}(S)_{|S}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{S}(-K_{S})
is ample.

From the exact sequence above we conclude that
\mathcal{N}_{S/\widetilde{W}}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{S}(-K_{S})
is ample.
∎

Proof of Prop.
7.5
.

For
i=0,\dotsc,r
let us consider the transform
T_{i}\subset{W}
of the quadric cone in
\mathbb{P}^{4}
containing
A
and with vertex
q_{i}
.

In
{W}
we have

T_{i}\sim 2H-\sum_{j=0}^{r}D_{j}-D_{i}
,
\sum_{i=0}^{r}T_{i}\sim 2(r+1)H-(r+2)\sum_{j=0}^{r}D_{j}
,

-K_{W}=5H-3\sum_{i=0}^{r}D_{i}
,
and

(r+2)(-K_{W})=(4-r)H+3\sum_{i=0}^{r}T_{i}
.
We also have
\alpha^{*}(T_{i})=\widetilde{T}_{i}+E
, where
\widetilde{T}_{i}\subset X
is again the transform of
T_{i}
, and
\alpha^{*}(-K_{W})=-K_{X}+E
, which gives

(7.9)

(r+2)(-K_{X})=(4-r)H_{X}+3\sum_{i=0}^{r}\widetilde{T}_{i}+(2r+1)E.

We have
-K_{X|E}
ample by Lemma
7.8
, and in the notation of Lemma
7.7
we have
\widetilde{T}=\widetilde{T}_{0}
and
-K_{X|\widetilde{T}_{0}}
ample. By considering the projection from
q_{i}
instead of
q_{0}
, again by Lemma
7.7
we get
-K_{X|\widetilde{T}_{i}}
ample for every
i=0,\dotsc,r
.

Since
\sigma^{*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{4}}(1)
is nef in
\widehat{W}
, for its transform
H
in
W
we have
H\cdot\Gamma\geq 0
for every irreducible curve
\Gamma
not contained in the indeterminacy locus of
W\dasharrow\widehat{W}
.

Let us consider an irreducible curve
C\subset X
. If
C\subset E
, or if
C\subset\widetilde{T}_{i}
for some
i
, then
-K_{X}\cdot C>0
by what precedes.
Assume that
C\not\subset E
and
C\not\subset\widetilde{T}_{i}
for every
i
, and let
C_{W}\subset W
be the transform of
C
. If
C\cap E=\emptyset
, then
-K_{X}\cdot C=-K_{W}\cdot C_{W}>0
. If
C\cap E\neq\emptyset
, then
C_{W}\cap S\neq\emptyset
, therefore
C_{W}
is not contained in the indeterminacy locus of the map
W\dasharrow\widehat{W}
. Then
H_{X}\cdot C=H\cdot C_{W}\geq 0
,
\widetilde{T}_{i}\cdot C\geq 0
for every
i
,
E\cdot C>0
, and finally
-K_{X}\cdot C>0
by (
7.9
).

This shows that
-K_{X}
is strictly nef. Moreover
one can check directly that

K_{X}^{4}>0
(see Table
7.2
), so that
-K_{X}
is big, and it is ample by the base point free theorem.
∎

\begin{array}[]{||c|c|c|c|c|c|c|c||}\hline\cr\hline\cr r&\rho_{X}&K_{X}^{4}&K_{X}^{2}\cdot c_{2}(X)&b_{4}(X)=h^{2,2}(X)&b_{3}(X)&h^{0}(X,-K_{X})&\chi(T_{X})\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr 0&3&303&174&5&0&66&4\\
\hline\cr 1&4&256&160&8&0&57&2\\
\hline\cr 2&5&210&144&12&0&48&0\\
\hline\cr 3&6&165&126&17&0&39&-2\\
\hline\cr 4&7&121&106&23&0&30&-4\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr\end{array}
